		<item>
		<title>The new AgentCore runtime: Elastic, optimized, and consistently fast starts</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/the-new-agentcore-runtime-elastic-optimized-and-consistently-fast-starts/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Evandro Franco]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 15:31:34 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4445c20377fed08a36ecf07e723f634a75d5d673</guid>

					<description>Today we are announcing the new AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore built for the speed, flexibility, and cost efficiency that production agents demand. It reclaims memory as sessions release it and delivers consistent cold starts regardless of image size or concurrency.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Agents are no longer experiments. They process claims, write and review code, coordinate across systems, and run for hours without supervision. As agents take on more complex, longer-running work, the infrastructure underneath them must evolve just as fast.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We built Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to help developers build, connect, and optimize agents securely at scale. AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, is the managed compute layer that gives developers a fully managed environment to deploy and run agents without building or maintaining infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Since launch, thousands of teams have used it to run production agents. Every conversation with those teams teaches us something about what agents need next: faster responsiveness as workloads scale, finer control over resource allocation, and economics that track actual usage precisely.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we are announcing the new AgentCore runtime, purpose-built for the speed, flexibility, and cost efficiency that production agents demand.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It brings better memory management, reclaiming memory as a session releases it instead of holding it at the peak. It also delivers consistent cold start times regardless of container size or agent concurrency. You get the serverless model you already liked, now more elastic. Memory is released back the instant a session ends, startup times stay consistent regardless of size or concurrency, and the bill tracks the work your agent does.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;From conversation to workload&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many agents started as chat bots: you asked, it answered, and the exchange ended in seconds. Then came coding agents that work for minutes to hours, holding context across many steps, running while you watch or step away. Now agents are becoming ambient, always on, triggered by events, running unattended, surfacing only when a job finishes or hits a decision that needs a person. And there are far more of them: no longer novelties but running everywhere. They are embedded in products, behind everyday features, and increasingly launched by other agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first version of AgentCore runtime built a strong foundation for this spectrum of agents: serverless, session isolation, scale to zero, and pay only for what you use. Today’s launch of the new runtime extends that foundation across the full spectrum, staying fast and consistent for interactive agents, and durable and affordable for long-running, more autonomous agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;What AgentCore runtime provides&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With AgentCore runtime, you can focus on the agent instead of worrying about the scalable infrastructure needed underneath it. Two things make that possible, and they’re the reasons customers reach for it:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You pay only for what you consume, and not for idle CPU waiting for I/O. Billing follows resource usage, so there’s no standing charge for capacity you provisioned “just in case.”&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The platform scales all the way down to zero. When an agent isn’t handling work, there’s nothing running and nothing to pay for. When work arrives, the platform gets you the capacity you need.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Together they make it cheap to keep many agents idle most of the time and even cheap to run one that stays busy. The consumption model bends to the workload instead of forcing the workload to bend to it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As agents move from short question-and-answer sessions to ambient, always-on work, that same model runs into two challenges.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Memory is expensive, and today you pay the peak.&lt;/strong&gt; A session holds on to memory from the moment it allocates it until the session ends, because nothing reclaims it along the way. This works when the allocated memory is used to serve subsequent resources without incurring the latency to fetch it again. However, a long-running or bursty agent keeps paying for its high point the whole time it runs, well after it has stopped using that memory. For an agent that spikes now and then but sits idle most of the day, that is the gap between paying for the peak around the clock and paying for the real usage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Startup times vary.&lt;/strong&gt; Every new session has to start before it can do any work, so fast, predictable startup is central to a good experience. It matters most when a person is waiting on an agent that paused for input and needs to resume. The catch is the hardware-enforced isolation these sessions depend on: a session that lands on an already-initialized environment starts in under 100 milliseconds, but keeping environments hot enough to guarantee that means holding compute in reserve. So most sessions begin with a cold start: booting a fresh environment, pulling the image, and initializing the agent before the first request runs. That latency penalty grows with image size and concurrency, and it’s worst under bursty traffic, exactly when most sessions arrive and the fewest ready environments remain. That inconsistency is what a waiting user feels.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The workarounds are heavy.&lt;/strong&gt; To cover both challenges, customers often build the machinery themselves: holding spare environments ready so requests avoid a cold start, optimizing memory allocation, and tearing it all down again to keep the bill in check. Keeping capacity ready ahead of demand is costly and complex for anyone to run. It reserves scarce compute whether or not that compute is working, and it still gives way when a burst outruns what was set aside. This is undifferentiated work, and none of it is the agent itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Benefits of the new AgentCore runtime&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The enhanced AgentCore runtime takes care of both challenges for you, starting with lower memory consumption tracked to what you use. The new runtime now starts each session from a small, efficient memory profile rather than a full provisioned footprint. Additional memory is allocated and paged in on demand as the workload needs it. Based on an analysis of allocation patterns across billions of sessions, we tuned the new runtime to reclaim memory when it goes cold and is unlikely to be accessed again. It no longer holds that memory until the session ends. With the original runtime, allocated memory remained held even if it wasn’t used by subsequent requests, so the usage tracked the high watermark. With the new runtime, memory that is released or goes cold is reclaimed, and the bill tracks those changes over the lifetime of the session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21766-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21766-1.png" alt="Chart comparing session memory: the original runtime holds the peak while the new runtime reclaims memory as it goes cold" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Session memory usage for the original runtime compared to the new runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Faster, more consistent cold starts come as a direct benefit of smaller profiles at startup. The enhanced runtime prepares the environment once, snapshots it, and restores that snapshot for each new instance. Because the snapshot stays small and consistent, so do the starts, no matter the image size or how much concurrency you run. Rather than repeating the boot-and-initialize work on every cold start, the platform restores an environment that is already up. The runtime now delivers consistent starts in a tight, predictable range.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What we measured.&lt;/strong&gt; To isolate what the platform itself adds to a cold start, we tested an empty echo agent that returns its input and calls no model and no tools. The timing reflects the runtime’s start path rather than any application work. A Python client on an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance in us-west-2 called agents in us-east-1 over the public internet with no virtual private cloud (VPC) peering, using the &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt; SDK. These are client-side numbers, so each one includes the round trip between the two AWS Regions on top of the platform’s own start time. We sent 5,000 cold invocations per agent across both versions and five image sizes, within default account quotas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Measured this way, the new runtime delivers a P75 cold start latency of about 2 seconds from a 200 MB image all the way to 2 GB, because image size has no effect on it. The original runtime’s latency, by contrast, rises with image size, from roughly 5.4 seconds to nearly 30 seconds.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To put this latency in perspective, it helps to separate cold start latency from what a user waits on. Start time is how long it takes to get a ready environment before your agent code handles its first request. It is not the time the agent spends working. In a production agent, most of the wall-clock time a user experiences comes from the agent loop and its model calls, often several seconds each. In our echo test, the agent’s own code ran in about 34 milliseconds at P75, so nearly everything here is platform start time. The new runtime makes the platform’s portion of the start time fast and predictable, which matters most when a person is waiting on an interactive agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A practical tip for interactive agents.&lt;/strong&gt; You can hide the start time almost entirely by beginning the session as soon as the user engages, for example when they open a chat, even before they type in the input box, rather than waiting for them to submit. The session warms while they are greeted and while they type their first request, so by the time they send that message, the environment is ready.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21766-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/17/ML-21766-2.png" alt="P75 cold start latency by image size: the new runtime stays near 2 seconds while the original rises from 5.4 to nearly 30 seconds" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: P75 cold start latency across image sizes for the original and new runtime&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;How the new runtime works&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The next generation of the runtime reworks how sessions use memory, how agents load, and what you pay for.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Page memory in on demand and reclaim it when it is freed.&lt;/strong&gt; Instead of holding on to a session’s peak memory after it’s allocated, the new runtime now backs the session with a smaller resident footprint and brings in more memory as the workload touches it. When your agent lets memory go, by releasing per-request buffers and by letting cached data expire between requests, the platform takes it back rather than letting it stay claimed until the session ends.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Load the agent once, then snapshot it.&lt;/strong&gt; When you create or update an instance of the new runtime, AgentCore launches your container and waits for it to report healthy, then captures a snapshot of the running environment. By that point, your one-time initialization has already run, so work such as loading model artifacts and fetching static config is baked into the snapshot. Every new instance then starts by restoring that snapshot rather than initializing from scratch. The expensive startup work is paid once, and each instance inherits it instantly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Keep the snapshot small and its size steady.&lt;/strong&gt; A naive snapshot of a running process captures far more than a restored instance needs, including caches and transient memory that pad the snapshot and make restore time grow with image size. The new runtime strips that excess, so the snapshot holds only the working state an instance needs to resume, not its full resident footprint. The result is a snapshot whose size stays roughly flat as the container image grows, and that is what holds restore latency steady across a wide range of image sizes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Higher rate, lower bill.&lt;/strong&gt; The new runtime bills you for the memory that&amp;nbsp; your agent uses, loaded on demand and reclaimed when idle, not for holding your whole container image in memory all session. You pay a higher rate but on far fewer GB-hours, and for most agents the footprint drops more than the rate rises, so the bill goes down.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;What’s next (coming soon)&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond what we shipped today, several capabilities are on the way to give you more choice over pricing, compute, compatibility, and control.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Committed baseline discounts.&lt;/strong&gt; Today’s consumption-based pricing stays and works well for spiky and scale-to-zero workloads. Alongside it, the new runtime will add a baseline pricing option: you reserve a memory floor for a session and burst above it on demand. Baseline pricing suits steady, always-active agent sessions that want predictable cost, while consumption pricing continues to provide greater elasticity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Larger compute and storage.&lt;/strong&gt; Expand your agent’s environment with more RAM, vCPU, and session storage.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;x86 support.&lt;/strong&gt; Run the agent, tool, or environment you already have with x86 microVMs. Teams whose code or dependencies target x86 can move an agent, a tool, or an execution environment to AgentCore as-is.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Greater lifecycle control.&lt;/strong&gt; Suspend and resume sessions with memory snapshotting. Attach to runtime hooks to serialize state before an active session terminates, so sessions can resume indefinitely.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scoped identity for unattended agents.&lt;/strong&gt; Unattended agents raise a question a chat turn never did: what is this agent allowed to do when no one is watching it act? Session context keys will give each session its own scoped identity, so an unattended agent, tool, or environment acts with exactly the permissions defined for it and nothing more.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h1&gt;Getting started&lt;/h1&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with the new runtime, set the &lt;code&gt;platformVersion&lt;/code&gt; parameter to &lt;code&gt;V2&lt;/code&gt; when you create or update a runtime. See the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/runtime-how-it-works.html#runtime-platform-versions" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt; for more details on using the runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can find samples in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/02-host-your-agent/01-runtime/01-hosting-agents/01-http-protocol" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore GitHub samples repo&lt;/a&gt;. An &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/02-host-your-agent/01-runtime/05-measure-your-runtime" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;accompanying load test example&lt;/a&gt; shows the new runtime’s consistent cold start latency in your own AWS account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Deploy Hugging Face models on Amazon SageMaker AI with coding agents</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/deploy-hugging-face-models-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai-with-coding-agents/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Dario Salvati]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 15:25:23 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker AI]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">65191e6d2a65531f451a23942300a367fbb852d5</guid>

					<description>Deploy production-ready Hugging Face models on Amazon SageMaker AI using six open-source agent skills. Point a coding agent at a model and get back a real-time endpoint with the right serving container, autoscaling, Amazon CloudWatch alarms, and a verified teardown path.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Deploying a Hugging Face model to production means making a dozen decisions: choosing the right serving container for the model’s architecture, confirming the current image tag for your AWS Region, and matching an instance type to the model’s memory footprint. Beyond infrastructure, you must wire autoscaling so you don’t burn GPU hours on an idle endpoint. You also set Amazon CloudWatch alarms that catch silent failures before your users do. After you’ve made those decisions, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; collapses that work into hours.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This kind of structured, repeatable work is exactly what coding agents, like &lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kiro&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://www.anthropic.com/claude-code" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Claude Code&lt;/a&gt;, are built for. It’s tempting to describe a model to deploy in a coding agent, walk away, and come back to a working endpoint. In practice, an unguided coding agent might make wrong decisions, producing endpoints that are fragile, costly, or quietly wrong. The problem gets worse for newer models, since their training data might not include the latest deployment knowledge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learn how to deploy production-ready Hugging Face models on SageMaker AI using agent skills. You install six skills from &lt;a href="https://github.com/huggingface/skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face Skills&lt;/a&gt;, point a coding agent at a Hugging Face model, and get back a real-time endpoint with autoscaling, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; alarms, the correct serving container from the &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/reference/available_images/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers (DLC)&lt;/a&gt; catalog, and a verified teardown path. Real-time endpoint is the default, but the skills also support real-time with scale-to-zero, serverless inference, asynchronous inference, batch transform, and Amazon Bedrock Custom Model Import. The skills are open source, use only Python and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI)&lt;/a&gt;, and work unchanged on macOS, Linux, and Windows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-problem-with-an-unguided-coding-agent"&gt;The problem with an unguided coding agent&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To show what the skills actually prevent, it helps to watch what a capable agent does without them. We tested both Kiro (with Auto or Claude Fable 5) and Claude Code (with Opus 4.8) for the request:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;deploy the small [Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B] (https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B) model to a real-time endpoint, write the plan to a file first, and keep a log of every action.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Both coding agents initially chose Text Generation Inference (TGI) as the serving container to deploy, an understandable choice given that TGI was the default for years and model training data is full of tutorials that reach it. But the TGI build available in the Region predated Qwen3’s architecture and couldn’t load the model. The endpoint failed its health check. The agent bumped the TGI version, redeployed, failed again, and pivoted to vLLM. This resulted in multiple deployment failures, each of which billed GPU time as it started and then crashed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The second request failed more quietly. We asked the same agent to deploy a multimodal mixture-of-experts (MoE) diffusion model released only weeks before the test. The coding agents confirmed it existed, and again wrote a script built on TGI, a text-generation server with no backend for a discrete-diffusion image-text model. Nothing failed loudly. You would find out only when the endpoint refused to come up.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two runs share the same root cause: missing deployment facts, not reasoning failure. The agent planned and debugged well. What it lacked was current, specific knowledge. Recent Qwen models need vLLM. Python 3.13 has no working wheels for much of the machine learning (ML) stack. Container images should be resolved from the published AWS Deep Learning Containers catalog. This knowledge changes faster than model weights get updated. So we make it into editable skill files rather than rely on the latest release of a model to absorb it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Table 1 compares the model deployment made by the unguided agent against the agent with skills installed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Deployment concerns&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Unguided agent&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With skills&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Serving container&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;TGI first → health-check failure → vLLM&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;vLLM, chosen before any resource was created&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Image URI&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Discovered by trial and error&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resolved from the AWS DLC catalog, with fallback when the registry query was denied&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Autoscaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Target tracking, 1–2 instances&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Monitoring&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Three CloudWatch alarms (latency, errors, overhead)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Documentation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;README recommended TGI, the SageMaker SDK, and Python 3.13&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Plan and scripts matched what actually ran&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Region, role, environment&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Correct natively&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Correct by rule&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Teardown&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A script you could run&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Run, then verified the resources were gone&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: The same request, run by the agent without and with the skills installed&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The rest of this post shows how we deploy Hugging Face models on SageMaker AI endpoints (the right-hand column of Table 1) using agent skills.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="agent-skills-for-deploying-hugging-face-models-on-sagemaker-ai"&gt;Agent skills for deploying Hugging Face models on SageMaker AI&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Six skills from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/huggingface/skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face Skills&lt;/a&gt; GitHub repo cover the end-to-end deployment workflow. The planner skill orchestrates the other five, as shown in the following diagram.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-text"&gt;hf-cloud-sagemaker-deployment-planner (orchestrate, ask only what's needed)
│
├── hf-cloud-aws-context-discovery (discover local AWS context)
├── hf-cloud-python-env-setup (set up an isolated Python environment)
├── hf-cloud-sagemaker-iam-preflight (verify a usable execution role)
├── hf-cloud-serving-image-selection (select the right container family and image URI)
└── hf-cloud-sagemaker-production-defaults (deploy with autoscaling, alarms, and tags)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="an-agent-skill-example"&gt;An agent skill example&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;An &lt;a href="https://agentskills.io/home" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agent skill&lt;/a&gt; is an open standard package consisting of a folder with a required SKILL.md file. This file includes metadata (name and description, at minimum) and instructions that tell an agent how to perform a specific task. Skills load through progressive disclosure. An agent reads a skill on demand when the current task matches its description. The following is a trimmed version of the &lt;code&gt;hf-cloud-serving-image-selection&lt;/code&gt; skill.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;---
name: hf-cloud-serving-image-selection
description: Pick the right serving container for a SageMaker model deployment and find its current image URI. Use this skill whenever about to deploy a model to a SageMaker endpoint and an image URI needs to be chosen --- including when the user says "deploy this LLM", "host this HuggingFace model", "serve this fine-tuned model", "deploy this embedding model", "host a reranker", "serve a sentence-transformers model", or when about to hardcode any container URI in deployment code. HuggingFace-curated Deep Learning Containers are ALWAYS preferred: HuggingFace vLLM (LLMs and generative rerankers), HuggingFace vLLM-Omni (multimodal), TEI (embeddings/cross-encoder rerankers), HF Inference Toolkit (other transformers). Generic images (AWS vLLM, DJL-LMI, SGLang) are used only when no HuggingFace image is compatible --- never merely because they carry a newer version. Never hardcode a container URI from memory and never default to TGI. Prevents stale-image failures and wrong-region URIs
---
Serving Image Selection
The serving container is the single thing most likely to break a deployment
that "looked correct on paper". Wrong container, stale tag, or wrong AMI all
produce the same opaque `Failed to pass health check` error.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="end-to-end-model-deployment-phases"&gt;End-to-end model deployment phases&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The skills drive five AWS services. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt; hosts the endpoint, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)&lt;/a&gt; provides the execution role. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ecr/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://aws.github.io/deep-learning-containers/reference/available_images/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers&lt;/a&gt; supply the serving image, while &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cloudwatch/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon CloudWatch&lt;/a&gt; powers the alarms. All helper scripts in skills call these services through Boto3 and the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI), which retains full control over what gets created. The SageMaker Python SDK works too, but the skills default to Boto3.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The deployment follows six phases:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Discover the AWS context (profile, Region, account, and caller identity) with read-only calls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set up an isolated Python environment with a supported Python version and a current &lt;code&gt;boto3&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Find an existing SageMaker AI execution role and create one only if none exists and you have permission.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select the serving container family and resolve a current image URI from the AWS DLC catalog.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create the model, endpoint configuration, and endpoint, and then attach autoscaling and Amazon CloudWatch alarms.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run a smoke test against the live endpoint and report the result.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To follow along, you need the following:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;strong&gt;AWS account&lt;/strong&gt; with permission to use Amazon SageMaker AI, including an existing SageMaker AI execution role. The skills can find one automatically or create one if none exists and your credentials allow it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS CLI v2&lt;/strong&gt;, configured with credentials for that account.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Python 3.10, 3.11, or 3.12&lt;/strong&gt;. Python 3.13 or later isn’t supported because much of the ML stack does not yet publish wheels for these versions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;coding agent&lt;/strong&gt; that supports skills. This post uses &lt;a href="https://kiro.dev/ide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Kiro IDE&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Git, to clone the skills repository.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post deploys &lt;code&gt;Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B&lt;/code&gt; to a single &lt;code&gt;ml.g5.xlarge&lt;/code&gt; real-time inference instance in &lt;code&gt;US East (N. Virginia) Region (us-east-1)&lt;/code&gt;. Confirm your account has available quota for this instance type before you start.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Note that a real-time endpoint bills continuously whether it serves traffic, so delete the endpoint when you’re done or follow the teardown steps at the end of this post.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="install-the-skills"&gt;Install the skills&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kiro supports two skill scopes: workspace and global. The workspace skills reside in your project under .kiro/skills/ and apply only to project-specific workflows. The global skills reside under ~/.kiro/skills/ and are available across all workspaces.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To install the six skills from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/huggingface/skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face Skills&lt;/a&gt; GitHub repo in the current workspace, enter the following request in a Kiro default agent chat session:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;Install six agent skills from the huggingface/skills repo, pinned to commit
f3186efbbc322121eb5d0f31e8a1d669ee961159, into this workspace.
Source: https://github.com/huggingface/skills.git
Commit: f3186efbbc322121eb5d0f31e8a1d669ee961159
Skills live under the repo's skills/ directory:
- hf-cloud-sagemaker-deployment-planner
- hf-cloud-aws-context-discovery
- hf-cloud-python-env-setup
- hf-cloud-sagemaker-iam-preflight
- hf-cloud-serving-image-selection
- hf-cloud-sagemaker-production-defaults&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Kiro summarizes the installed files as shown in Figure 1. Note that this post tested and used the repo with a specific SHA: &lt;code&gt;f3186efbbc322121eb5d0f31e8a1d669ee961159&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21283-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21283-1.png" alt="Kiro chat session confirming the six agent skills installed in the workspace" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Kiro finishes installing the six agent skills&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To confirm all six skill directories are present, enter &lt;code&gt;/&lt;/code&gt; in the Kiro chat session to see available skills as slash commands, as shown in Figure 2.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21283-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21283-2.png" alt="Kiro chat session listing the six installed skills as slash commands" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Enter / to see available skills in the Kiro chat session&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deploy-a-model-with-kiro"&gt;Deploy a model with Kiro&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the skills installed, you describe the model to the agent in plain language, and the planner skill takes over. You don’t specify which container family to use, how to find the execution role, or which production defaults to attach, because those decisions live in the skills.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Enter the following request in the Kiro chat session:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;I need to deploy a model on AWS SageMaker, and I don't want to deal with all the console selecting and boto3 myself. The model is Qwen3 0.6B, pinned to commit `c1899de289a04d12100db370d81485cdf75e47ca`, called from an internal app. Figure out the best way to deploy it and walk me through it. Write the plan to a file first, and keep a log of every action you take.&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy the model, complete the following steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review the plan.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent writes a deployment plan to a file and waits for your approval before creating any billable resources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS context discovery and container selection.&lt;/strong&gt; The agent discovers the AWS context (profile, Region, account). The &lt;code&gt;hf-cloud-serving-image-selection&lt;/code&gt; skill selects vLLM for Qwen3 and resolves the image URI from the AWS DLC catalog.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Approve the deployment&lt;/strong&gt; when the agent asks. The &lt;code&gt;hf-cloud-sagemaker-production-defaults&lt;/code&gt; skill creates the model, endpoint configuration, and endpoint as a unit, then attaches autoscaling and CloudWatch alarms.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Verify.&lt;/strong&gt; Review the smoke-test result the agent reports after the endpoint reaches &lt;code&gt;InService&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following lines come from the deployment log the agent kept during the run:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-plaintext"&gt;## Step 5: Serving Image Selection
| Value | Resolved to |
| Image URI | `763104351884.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/huggingface-vllm:0.28.0-transformers5.15.0-gpu-py312-cu130-ubuntu24.04` |
| InferenceAmiVersion | `al2-ami-sagemaker-inference-gpu-3-1` |
| `SM_VLLM_MODEL` | `Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B` |
| `SM_VLLM_HOST` | `0.0.0.0` (else vLLM binds localhost, ping fails, container dies) |
| `SM_VLLM_TRUST_REMOTE_CODE` | `false` |
| `SM_VLLM_MAX_MODEL_LEN` | `8192` |&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a gated model, add a &lt;code&gt;HUGGING_FACE_HUB_TOKEN&lt;/code&gt; environment variable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="resolve-the-execution-role"&gt;Resolve the execution role&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deployments often stop at the execution role when calling &lt;code&gt;iam:CreateRole&lt;/code&gt; fails on a corporate account whose AWS IAM Identity Center session has no IAM write access. The &lt;code&gt;hf-cloud-sagemaker-iam-preflight&lt;/code&gt; skill reverses the order: find first, create only as a last resort.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Its &lt;code&gt;check_role.py&lt;/code&gt; script searches the account for existing roles that match patterns such as &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMaker-ExecutionRole-*&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;*SageMaker*Execution*&lt;/code&gt;, and ranks them by last-used date. It also validates the trust policy and returns the Amazon Resource Name (ARN). It creates a role only when none exists and the caller has &lt;code&gt;iam:CreateRole&lt;/code&gt; permission. Note that the created role carries &lt;code&gt;AmazonSageMakerFullAccess&lt;/code&gt;. We recommend updating the role to grant only the permissions it needs, following the principle of least privilege.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="apply-production-defaults"&gt;Apply production defaults&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;hf-cloud-sagemaker-production-defaults&lt;/code&gt; skill turns an endpoint from a demo into a production deployment. It applies the defaults in Table 2 to every endpoint, establishing an operational baseline. For production deployment, you need to add user-specific configurations, such as Amazon Virtual Private Cloud and AWS Key Management Service configurations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resource&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Name&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Billing&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Model&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;qwen3-06b-internal&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;none&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Endpoint config&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;qwen3-06b-internal-20260904-1913-config&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;none&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Endpoint&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;qwen3-06b-internal-20260904-1913&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;$1.408/hr per instance&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Autoscaling target + policy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;endpoint/.../variant/AllTraffic&lt;/code&gt;, min 1 max 4&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;none&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CloudWatch alarms x3&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;endpoint&amp;gt;-Invocation5XXErrors&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;-ModelLatencyP99&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;-OverheadLatencyP99&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;negligible&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 2: Production defaults the skill applies to every endpoint&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decisions-the-coding-agent-made-on-its-own"&gt;Decisions the coding agent made on its own&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agent skills define the workflow, but the coding agent still makes judgment calls within it. In one case, when the agent queried Amazon ECR for the newest image tag, the call was denied because the IAM Identity Center role lacked ecr-public:DescribeImages permission. Rather than fail the deployment, the agent fell back to the known-good tag the skill ships as a safety net and recorded the reason in the log. In another case, after the smoke test returned HTTP 200, the agent noticed that the actual answer was never emitted. This is because the model’s reply was truncated at max_tokens while still inside the Qwen3 reasoning block. The agent flagged this in the log as a configuration issue: the calling application should raise the token limit rather than treat the test as a pass.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A real-time endpoint bills for its instance the entire time it exists. Delete the resources you created to avoid ongoing charges. The &lt;code&gt;sagemaker-production-defaults&lt;/code&gt; skill includes a &lt;code&gt;teardown.py&lt;/code&gt; script that removes the deployment resources and then confirms they’re gone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To clean up, complete the following steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Ask the agent to tear down the deployment or run &lt;code&gt;teardown.py&lt;/code&gt; directly with the endpoint name and Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Confirm the script reports the endpoint, endpoint configuration, and model as deleted. The script verifies the deletion.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Check that the autoscaling policy and CloudWatch alarms have been removed. Delete any that remain.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Six reusable agent skills turn an unguided coding agent into one that deploys Hugging Face models on SageMaker AI endpoints with production-ready features. Each deployment includes the proper container, autoscaling, CloudWatch alarms, and a verified teardown path. Without these skills, agents reach for outdated containers, skip production safeguards, and leave misleading documentation. This post walks through each skill: AWS context discovery, Python environment setup, IAM role resolution, container selection from the AWS DLC catalog, and deployment with production defaults.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, install the skills from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/huggingface/skills" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Hugging Face Skills&lt;/a&gt; GitHub repo and deploy your first model. The skills are open source and contributions are welcome.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Teams can also use Amazon SageMaker JumpStart to deploy a set of popular Hugging Face models directly from the console, and Inference Recommendations to automatically benchmark and select the optimal instance type for their workload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="learn-more"&gt;Learn more&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/latest/dg/realtime-endpoints.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker AI Developer Guide – Real-time inference&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/deep-learning-containers/latest/devguide/what-is-dlc.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Deep Learning Containers documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Introducing Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference-gateway/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Vinay Arora]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 18 Sep 2026 13:08:34 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker HyperPod]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Expert (400)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">f7030591b5a0a92a50f229929b93194a36691b5e</guid>

					<description>Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway is a Kubernetes-native, GPU-aware routing add-on for Amazon EKS. It uses real-time GPU signals to send each inference request to the best-suited pod, cutting first-token latency by up to 82% with no changes to your model servers or client applications.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Eliminate GPU waste. Reduce first-token latency by up to 82%. Install one Kubernetes-native addon with zero application changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-problem-naive-routing-wastes-your-most-expensive-resource"&gt;The problem: Naive routing wastes your most expensive resource&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Running large language models (LLMs) at scale on GPU clusters is expensive. The default Kubernetes load balancers are making it worse. Round-robin and least-connections algorithms have no visibility into what’s happening inside your GPUs: which pods have saturated KV caches, which are mid-way through long-context generations, or which already have the LoRA adapter your request needs loaded in memory.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;The result? Round-robin routing causes requests to pile up behind busy pods while idle capacity remains unused.&lt;/em&gt; First-token latency spikes to 4+ seconds during traffic bursts. GPU utilization becomes uneven and unpredictable. You over-provision to compensate. This burns money on GPUs that aren’t doing useful work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-solution-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference-gateway"&gt;The solution: SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we’re excited to announce Amazon SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway. It is a Kubernetes-native, GPU-aware routing system that deploys as a single EKS managed addon on your existing HyperPod infrastructure. It uses real-time GPU signals to place every inference request on the best-suited pod, delivering lower latency with no changes to your model servers or client applications.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“A chatbot user waiting 4.4 seconds for the first token now sees it in under 800 ms.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works-two-tier-architecture"&gt;How it works: Two-tier architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Inference Gateway uses a two-tier design built entirely on Kubernetes-native primitives.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21822-1-1.png" alt="Inference Gateway two-tier architecture with Envoy Gateway, Body-Based Router, and Endpoint Picker routing to model pods" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Two-tier architecture of the Inference Gateway&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tier-1-per-cluster-gateway-eks-managed-addon"&gt;Tier 1 — Per-cluster gateway (EKS managed addon)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first tier installs directly on each HyperPod/EKS cluster as the amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference addon. It consists of three core components, all built on the open-source Gateway API Inference Extension:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="envoy-gateway"&gt;1. Envoy Gateway&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;High-performance L7 proxy that terminates incoming HTTPS traffic and exposes a single private endpoint per cluster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="body-based-router-bbr"&gt;2. Body-Based Router (BBR)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inspects each incoming OpenAI-compatible request body, extracts the model field, and routes to the correct model pool. Supports multi-model routing: one gateway, many models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="endpoint-picker-epp"&gt;3. Endpoint Picker (EPP)&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The intelligence layer. EPP consumes real-time Prometheus metrics from every model-serving pod and uses a weighted scoring algorithm to select the best-suited backend:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;KV cache utilization —&lt;/strong&gt; avoids pods whose key-value memory is nearly full.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Queue depth —&lt;/strong&gt; avoids pods with deep request backlogs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;LoRA adapter residency —&lt;/strong&gt; prefers pods that already have the requested adapter loaded.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Prefix cache hit rate —&lt;/strong&gt; prefers pods likely to serve from cached prompt prefixes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Running requests —&lt;/strong&gt; balances active work across the fleet.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each scorer carries a configurable weight, so you can tune routing behavior for your specific workload (latency-sensitive chat compared to throughput-optimized batch).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="tier-2-global-inference-router-gir-coming-soon"&gt;Tier 2 — Global Inference Router (GIR) (coming soon)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The second tier adds fleet-wide coordination across multiple clusters and regions, with cross-cluster failover, global rate limiting, and cost-aware traffic shaping. Tier 2 builds on top of Tier 1. Each cluster’s per-cluster gateway continues to handle local intelligent routing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="getting-started-in-5-minutes"&gt;Getting started in 5 minutes&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Inference Gateway deploys with a single addon install and a declarative InferenceGatewayConfig custom resource. No sidecars, no service mesh, no application code changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-install-the-addon"&gt;Step 1: Install the addon&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws eks create-addon \
  --cluster-name my-hyperpod-cluster \
  --addon-name amazon-sagemaker-hyperpod-inference \
  --addon-version v2.0.0-eksbuild.1 \
  --configuration-values '{"inferenceGateway": {"enabled": true}, "inferenceOperator": {"enabled": true}}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-label-your-model-pods"&gt;Step 2: Label your model pods&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Add a label to your existing model server deployments so the gateway can discover them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;spec:
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: vllm-llama # The gateway matches on this&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-apply-the-gateway-configuration"&gt;Step 3: Apply the gateway configuration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create a single InferenceGatewayConfig resource that defines your models and routing behavior:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-yaml"&gt;apiVersion: inference.sagemaker.aws.amazon.com/v1alpha1
kind: InferenceGatewayConfig
metadata:
  name: my-gateway
spec:
  tls: {}
  bbr:
    enabled: true
  schedulers:
    - name: llama-70b
      modelName: "llama-3.1-70b"
      modelSelector:
        matchLabels:
          app: vllm-llama
      targetPort: 8000
      scheduler: llm-d&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-send-inference-requests"&gt;Step 4: Send inference requests&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway exposes a standard OpenAI-compatible endpoint. Your existing client code works unchanged:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;curl -X POST "http://&amp;lt;gateway-endpoint&amp;gt;/v1/chat/completions" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"model": "llama-3.1-70b", "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": "What is Kubernetes?"}], "max_tokens": 100}'&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That’s it. No SDK changes. No SigV4 signing for inference traffic. Standard HTTP with an OpenAI-compatible schema.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="multi-model-routing"&gt;Multi-model routing&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Running multiple models on the same cluster? The Body-Based Router handles it natively. Define multiple schedulers in your config, and the gateway automatically routes each request to the correct model pool based on the model field in the request body.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;One gateway. Multiple models. Zero routing logic in your application.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lora-adapter-routing"&gt;LoRA adapter routing&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Serving fine-tuned LoRA adapters on a shared base model? The Inference Gateway routes adapter requests to pods that already have the adapter loaded in GPU memory. This eliminates costly adapter swap latency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The EPP’s LoRA Affinity Scorer identifies which pods have the requested adapter resident and routes accordingly. If no pod has it loaded, the request goes to the pod with the most available capacity to load it quickly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="graceful-failure-and-self-healing"&gt;Graceful failure and self-healing&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway degrades gracefully at every level:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Failure Scope&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Behavior&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recovery&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pod failure&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;EPP excludes pods with stale metrics. Routes to healthy pods&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Automatic when metrics resume&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pool exhaustion&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Returns HTTP 429 with Retry-After header&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Autoscaling adds capacity&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cluster failure&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;GIR detects stale heartbeat, redirects traffic within 35s&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Gradual ramp-up on reintroduction&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regional failure&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cross-region routing activates automatically&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Higher latency, no availability impact&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="built-in-observability"&gt;Built-in observability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway emits metrics at every layer, all surfaced through your existing monitoring stack:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pod level —&lt;/strong&gt; KV cache utilization, queue depth, running requests, adapter residency (Prometheus).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Pool level —&lt;/strong&gt; Request totals, duration histograms, token counts (Prometheus/Grafana).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cluster level —&lt;/strong&gt; Average KV cache, error rate, P99 latency (Amazon CloudWatch).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fleet level —&lt;/strong&gt; Routing decisions, failover events, rate limit hits (CloudWatch).&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="benchmarking"&gt;Benchmarking&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;How much performance are you leaving on the table with default Kubernetes routing? To find out, we benchmarked four models ranging from 8B to 235B parameters, deployed on p5.48xlarge (H100) and g5 (A10G) instances. All traffic was routed through internal Application Load Balancers, matching the exact path a production request travels. A dedicated client node group generated controlled load while model servers ran in isolation on a separate server node group, ensuring zero resource contention under high concurrency. Every result that follows uses the gateway’s default routing configuration, with no tuning required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;GPU-aware routing delivers the biggest gains exactly where round-robin struggles most. We tested three real-world scenarios across four models (8B to 235B parameters).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="mixed-gpu-generations"&gt;Mixed GPU generations&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In production, GPU fleets are rarely uniform. When a smaller-memory instance saturates under traffic that its larger peers handle comfortably, round-robin keeps sending requests to overloaded pods. The Inference Gateway detects this imbalance in real time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21822-2-1.png" alt="Bar chart comparing first-token latency on mixed GPU generations for the round-robin baseline and the Inference Gateway" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Mixed GPU generations, round-robin baseline compared to the Inference Gateway&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="bursty-traffic"&gt;Bursty traffic&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bursty demand is the norm for most LLM workloads. A single replica cycles between overloaded and idle, creating latency spikes that round-robin cannot smooth out. The gateway absorbs these bursts by steering requests toward pods with available capacity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21822-3-1.png" alt="Bar chart comparing first-token latency under bursty traffic for the round-robin baseline and the Inference Gateway" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Bursty traffic, round-robin baseline compared to the Inference Gateway&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="shared-prompt-prefixes"&gt;Shared prompt prefixes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Workloads like multi-turn conversations and document Q&amp;amp;A share a common prompt prefix across requests. The gateway’s Prefix Cache Hit Rate scorer routes these requests to pods that already have the prefix cached, avoiding redundant computation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21822-4-1.png" alt="Bar chart comparing first-token latency with shared prompt prefixes for the round-robin baseline and the Inference Gateway" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Shared prompt prefixes, round-robin baseline compared to the Inference Gateway&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern across all three scenarios is consistent: the more your fleet diverges from uniform, the more you gain. On a fully uniform fleet under steady traffic, replicas hold near-identical utilization, and the gateway performs on par with round-robin. This makes intelligent routing most valuable for the conditions production traffic actually creates: mixed hardware, bursty demand, and shared prompt prefixes. You can turn it on without hand-tuning anything first.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="summary-of-performance-improvements"&gt;Summary of performance improvements&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;All figures are measured against a Kubernetes round-robin baseline on the same model replicas, using the gateway’s default routing configuration.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workload Condition&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TTFT P95&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;TTFT P99&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Throughput&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mixed GPU generations (Llama-3.1-8B)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–97%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–97%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+8%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Mixed GPU generations (Qwen3-32B)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–98%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–97%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+50%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bursty traffic (Llama-3.1-70B)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–94%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–98%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+12%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bursty traffic (Qwen3-235B)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Comparable&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–89%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Comparable&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Shared prompt prefix (Llama-3.1-8B)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–26%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;–43%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Comparable&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Uniform fleet, steady traffic (Qwen3-235B)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Comparable&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Comparable&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Comparable&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Comparable” means the difference fell within run-to-run variance.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="why-kubernetes-native-matters"&gt;Why Kubernetes-native matters&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Inference Gateway is not a separate platform you deploy alongside Kubernetes. It is Kubernetes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Gateway API conformant —&lt;/strong&gt; built on the official Kubernetes Gateway API and its Inference Extension.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Declarative config —&lt;/strong&gt; one CRD (InferenceGatewayConfig) defines your entire routing topology.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No lock-in —&lt;/strong&gt; works with any OpenAI-compatible model server (vLLM, SGLang, TGI, and so on).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Existing tooling —&lt;/strong&gt; kubectl, GitOps, Helm, ArgoCD — all work as expected.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;EKS addon lifecycle —&lt;/strong&gt; install, upgrade, and rollback through the aws eks CLI or console.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="availability"&gt;Availability&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;SageMaker HyperPod Inference Gateway (Tier 1, per-cluster routing) is available today in regions where inference add-on is available.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="whats-next"&gt;What’s next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tier 2: Global Inference Router (GIR) —&lt;/strong&gt; Cross-cluster and cross-region intelligent routing with a centralized fleet gateway, global rate limiting, and cost-tier-aware traffic shaping.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Canary traffic splitting —&lt;/strong&gt; Route a percentage of traffic to new model versions using InferenceModelRewrite CRDs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Flow control and priority bands —&lt;/strong&gt; Classify requests as Critical, Standard, or Sheddable with per-band admission control.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Ready to stop wasting GPU capacity on naive routing? Install the Inference Gateway addon and start seeing first-token latency improvements in minutes rather than weeks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Reduce time-to-hire for quality candidates with AI-powered Amazon Connect Talent</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reduce-time-to-hire-for-quality-candidates-with-ai-powered-amazon-connect-talent/</link>
					
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Ayesha Borker]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 17 Sep 2026 17:55:20 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Connect]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">a6559b315a35bac095e4d57dabbff35e09f15496</guid>

					<description>Amazon Connect Talent is an AI hiring solution built for talent acquisition leaders managing scaled hiring. It delivers AI-led interviews, data-driven assessments, and consistent evaluation, helping recruiters identify strong candidates more efficiently while providing applicants with a flexible interview experience. Informed by decades of Amazon's hiring science, Amazon Connect Talent provides transparency for every assessment, interview, and candidate score, enabling recruiters to stay in control of final hiring decisions.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Hiring at scale in industries such as retail, logistics, hospitality, and others has its fair share of challenges. Recruiting teams are expected to fill hundreds of roles within tight timelines, often with limited capacity and with tools that weren’t designed to seamlessly work together. As a result of this, applications pile up, phone screens get delayed, and strong candidates move on before anyone has a chance to reach out to them. The cost of this isn’t just operational. Every unfilled role slows production, impacts customer experience, and affects revenue. Most organizations are stitching together an Applicant Tracking System (ATS), a scheduling tool, spreadsheets, and disconnected feedback loops to manage a process that demands speed and coordination across every step. The hiring workflow itself becomes the bottleneck.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Today, we’re launching &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/products/connect/talent/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Connect Talent,&lt;/a&gt; an AI hiring solution built for talent acquisition leaders managing scaled hiring. It delivers AI-led interviews, data-driven assessments, and consistent evaluation, helping recruiters identify strong candidates more efficiently while providing applicants with a flexible interview experience. Informed by decades of Amazon’s hiring science, Amazon Connect Talent provides transparency for every assessment, interview, and candidate score, enabling recruiters to stay in control of final hiring decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Recruiters configure the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/talent/latest/userguide/evaluations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;evaluation criteria&lt;/a&gt;, assessments, and interview questions based on job requirements. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/talent/latest/userguide/ai-led-interviews.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AI agents then conduct interviews&lt;/a&gt; and assessments at scale, processing thousands of candidates across the hiring pipeline. For candidates, the experience is flexible. AI agents are available day or night, so a candidate can complete their interview at 9 PM after putting the kids to bed, from any device, without scheduling delays. For recruiters, the next morning &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/talent/latest/userguide/review-candidate-results.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;brings a dashboard&lt;/a&gt; of scored candidates, complete transcripts, and clear evaluation reasoning for every score, ready for review and final decision. This is human-AI collaboration applied to hiring. AI handles the screening and evaluation, and recruiters focus on what matters most, making informed hiring decisions with the full picture in front of them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone wp-image-139627 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-1.png" alt="" width="3024" height="1800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Amazon Connect Talent lets recruiters configure AI-led interviews and candidate assessments in minutes, tailored to each role.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt; &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-2-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone wp-image-139629 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-2-1.png" alt="" width="2124" height="1270"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Candidates experience a consistent, structured AI-led interview — available whenever they’re ready, day or night.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Informed by decades of Amazon’s hiring science&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon is one of the world’s largest employers, and Amazon Connect Talent puts decades of Amazon’s hiring science to work for organizations, with a highly configurable solution that adapts to specific hiring requirements. With consistent, evidence-based assessments applied to every candidate, organizations can interview more people than ever before, surface qualified candidates that might have been missed, and make better hiring decisions at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Reducing human preconceptions in hiring&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Connect Talent’s AI focuses exclusively on measuring a candidate’s job-related competencies, testing abilities including problem-solving, logic, listening, and role-specific capabilities. All candidate data is anonymized during AI evaluation, removing factors that can introduce unconscious human preconceptions. The assessments are designed around competencies associated with success in the role, not subjective impressions like confidence level or speaking style.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Transparency and consistent evaluation standards&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Connect Talent communicates clearly to both recruiters and candidates about what data is collected, how it is used, and what is not collected. Candidates are informed of what to expect before they proceed to take the evaluation. Each competency is scored against a rubric that defines what a strong response and a weak response look like, and every score is tied to specific evidence from the interview so the result can be traced back to what the candidate said. Traditionally, hiring teams can only evaluate a fraction of applicants, but Connect Talent changes that. With consistent, evidence-backed assessments applied to every candidate, whether evaluating the first or the thousandth, organizations get the same standard of evaluation across the entire pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human in the loop: recruiter control at every step&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Recruiters maintain final decision authority over every hire. Connect Talent gives them scored candidate summaries with competency breakdowns, complete interview transcripts, comparative analytics, and clear reasoning behind every score, all in a single view instead of pieced together from different places. Recruiters can pull up full transcripts and notes at any time, review how the AI scored a candidate, and make the final hiring decision with confidence that speed hasn’t come at the cost of quality. A retail organization ramping up 500 warehouse roles for peak season, for example, can have AI agents interview candidates around the clock while recruiters walk in each morning to a dashboard of structured, evidence-based evaluations ready for their review and final call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone wp-image-139630 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-3.png" alt="" width="3022" height="1744"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Amazon Connect Talent lets recruiters configure AI-led interviews and candidate assessments in minutes, tailored to each role.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone wp-image-139631 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-4.png" alt="" width="3022" height="1744"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img loading="lazy" class="alignnone wp-image-139632 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ConnectTalent-5.png" alt="" width="3022" height="1744"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Amazon Connect Talent lets recruiters review candidate responses and evaluation scores with details and full transparency&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enterprise-grade security built on AWS infrastructure&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Hiring data is sensitive. Candidate information deserves the same level of protection&amp;nbsp;you’d&amp;nbsp;expect for financial records or health information. Amazon Connect Talent delivers enterprise-grade security built on AWS infrastructure:&amp;nbsp;the same foundation trusted by banks, hospitals, and government agencies worldwide.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Built-in security controls help protect candidate data, with rigorous measures to meet your requirements. Access is controlled, auditable, and configurable. When your candidates share their information, they can trust&amp;nbsp;it’s&amp;nbsp;protected.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Integrity monitoring and fraud protection:&amp;nbsp;&lt;/strong&gt;Amazon Connect Talent uses&amp;nbsp;text-based&amp;nbsp;analysis to flag unnatural cadence, filler words, pauses, and response latency.&amp;nbsp;Human review is mandatory for every flag. No candidate is disqualified by automated signals alone.&amp;nbsp;Amazon Connect Talent provides signals, never verdicts. Recruiters&amp;nbsp;maintain&amp;nbsp;full control over how to handle flagged interviews.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Audit trails and explainability:&amp;nbsp;&lt;/strong&gt;Every&amp;nbsp;candidate&amp;nbsp;interaction is logged with a complete audit trail and clear job-related evaluation reasoning. The system includes ongoing monitoring and tracking to help your organization&amp;nbsp;demonstrate&amp;nbsp;best practices and meet requirements. When you make a hiring decision, you can show exactly how that decision was reached.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Improved business outcomes&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Connect Talent improves business outcomes by getting recruiters out of the workflow management cycle and empowering them with faster decision-making. Candidates get a faster and more flexible hiring experience. Organizations fill roles before lost revenue, rising costs, and competitive disadvantage set in. AI leads the interviewing while recruiters make the final decisions backed by data. This is human-AI collaboration where it matters.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Your business moves fast and now your hiring can too.&lt;/p&gt;
