   &lt;td&gt;CrewAI&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Self-Hosted/ CrewAI-Cloud&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Free/ Cloud&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Limited&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Basic&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Community&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;Self-Hosted/ Cloud&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Free/ Pro&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Moderate&lt;/td&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;Community + Pro&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Frontier Model Direct&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Provider-hosted&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-token&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Provider-Managed&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Provider SLA&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS managed&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Pay-per-use&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Yes&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Automatic scaling&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Native Amazon Bedrock Guardrails and Policy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Enterprise SLA&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore provides us a managed platform rather than a library we would need to operate ourselves. It works with any open source framework, including Strands Agents, LangGraph, LangChain, LlamaIndex, CrewAI, Google ADK, OpenAI Agents SDK, and with any model whether or not it runs on Amazon Bedrock. It supports both the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and the Agent-to-Agent (A2A) protocol. That meant we didn’t need to choose between open source flexibility and AWS service security. This allowed us to standardize the platform layer while leaving each team free to pick its own framework and model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Five capabilities decided it for us:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Managed infrastructure.&lt;/strong&gt; There’s no cluster to operate. AWS handles scaling, patching, and availability, so our platform team focuses on agent capabilities instead of backend plumbing. AgentCore reached general availability (GA) in October 2025, and at GA all services added support for Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (VPC), AWS PrivateLink, AWS CloudFormation, and resource tagging.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;True model agnosticism.&lt;/strong&gt; Claude, GPT-4.1, Amazon Nova, Mistral, and Llama are all reachable through a single platform. A team can plan with one model and execute with another, run a price-performance test by swapping providers, or move off a model that shipped a regression, without losing conversation context and without touching business logic.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automatic scaling.&lt;/strong&gt; AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, goes from zero to thousands of concurrent agent invocations with no capacity planning, with complete session isolation and execution windows up to 8 hours.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Native guardrails and identity.&lt;/strong&gt; Content filtering, personally identifiable information (PII) detection, and policy enforcement are managed capabilities rather than something each team reimplements. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy&lt;/a&gt; in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore integrates with the Gateway to intercept every tool call in real time and converts natural-language rules into Cedar, the AWS open source policy language, so development, compliance, and security teams can author and audit rules without custom code. AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, adds AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) integration, VPC isolation, and encryption, so agents support your compliance requirements and can act on behalf of a user or on their own under defined access controls.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Enterprise Support.&lt;/strong&gt; AWS Enterprise Support provides 24/7 support as well as a technical account manager (TAM) and account team relationship that helps customers influence the roadmap for products like AgentCore based on our needs.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;consumption-based pricing&lt;/a&gt; with no upfront commitments or minimum fees. Each service is billed independently, so a team can adopt a single capability such as AgentCore memory, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, without migrating its entire agent. Runtime billing is based on active CPU and memory consumption calculated per second, and CPU charges don’t accrue during I/O wait. This matters for agentic workloads, which typically spend 30–70 percent of their time waiting on model responses, tool calls, or database queries. With pre-allocated compute you would pay for that idle time. With AgentCore, you do not.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;APEX is organized in layers, shown in Figure 1. At the top, end users interact with three applications: Woody, Lens AI, and the ST Trading App. Those applications talk to the APEX frontend, which exposes a framework so product teams can drop agentic capabilities into a user interface without rebuilding the connection to the backend each time. The APEX backend is where AgentCore does the work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two infrastructure tracks sit alongside the backend. The WM infrastructure as code (IaC) Framework uses the AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) and GitHub to provision and version platform resources, with guardrails defined in code. The MCP layer connects the platform to third-party and external access, including AWS Marketplace and partner systems, over a standard protocol rather than bespoke integrations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Users reach three applications through the APEX frontend SDK. The APEX backend runs on AgentCore (Runtime, Identity, Gateway, Memory, Observability) with an Orchestrator, a vector database for retrieval, model access through the Amazon Bedrock model catalog, and Amazon Bedrock Guardrails. The WM IaC Framework provisions resources through AWS CDK and GitHub, and the MCP layer connects external systems.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-1.png" alt="APEX layered architecture showing three applications calling the APEX frontend SDK, the AgentCore backend, and the WM IaC and MCP tracks" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: APEX reference architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inside the backend, a request flows through these components.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;User Authentication and AgentCore Identity&lt;/strong&gt; establish who is calling and what they are entitled to do. Identity and entitlements are woven into every agent invocation rather than checked once at the edge, so an agent acting on a user’s behalf carries that user’s permissions through every downstream tool and data call. Federation to your identity provider of choice is also important. We use Okta as our source of truth.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The Orchestrator&lt;/strong&gt; routes the request and consults the Woodmac Agent Registry, a single place to discover, share, and reuse agents, tools, and skills across the organization with built-in governance and approval workflows. This is how one team’s agent becomes available to another without copying code.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore runtime&lt;/strong&gt; hosts the agent code in a serverless, session-isolated environment. Inside the runtime we run AI Agents Studio (built on Amazon Quick with Strands Agents, LangGraph, CrewAI, n8n, Vertex, and OpenAI), so each team keeps its preferred framework. The runtime invokes models through the Amazon Bedrock model catalog, which is where model agnosticism becomes concrete: the agent points at a model by name and we swap providers without rewriting the agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tools&lt;/strong&gt; are exposed to the agent as composable building blocks. The architecture shows how Wood Mackenzie uses a Code Interpreter tool for secure code execution and an Amazon Nova Act web tool for browser actions. These tools are reached through &lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Gateway&lt;/strong&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, which turns APIs, AWS Lambda functions, and existing MCP servers into agent-compatible tools and provides a single secure endpoint for discovery and invocation. The Gateway pulls from a Tools Repository for discovery and invocation, and supports IAM, OAuth 2.1, and API key authentication.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) over a vector database&lt;/strong&gt; grounds the agent in our own content, including unstructured documents, so responses are based on retrieved facts rather than the model’s parametric memory alone. We use &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt; as a serverless and managed way to provide RAG to our agents.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore memory&lt;/strong&gt; gives the agent short-term and long-term context, so a conversation persists across sessions and agents can share state instead of each maintaining its own store.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails and Policy&lt;/strong&gt; govern allowed prompts and intercept tool calls, enforcing content filtering, PII detection, and the Cedar-based rules described earlier.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Observability and evaluation&lt;/strong&gt; determine whether an agent survives in production. Agent output is non-deterministic, so the hard question isn’t &lt;em&gt;does it work&lt;/em&gt; but &lt;em&gt;can we tell when it stops working&lt;/em&gt;. AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, emits telemetry in OpenTelemetry-compatible format to Amazon CloudWatch, covering session count, latency, duration, token usage, and error rates. Wood Mackenzie can then trace a single request from a session down to an individual span, with logs from each component surfaced at the right step instead of scattered across log groups. On top of that, AgentCore Evaluations, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, gives us built-in evaluators for quality dimensions such as helpfulness, tool selection, and accuracy, plus custom model-based scoring, so we can score agents against real traffic.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="apex-studio-the-control-plane"&gt;APEX Studio: The control plane&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture described earlier is the runtime. APEX Studio is the back office that operates it, a single interface where a team builds, deploys, and monitors agents without touching the underlying infrastructure. It’s the human-facing control plane over AgentCore, and it’s how a team gets from idea to a live agent without filing a ticket for each step.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Studio dashboard, shown in Figure 2, surfaces the platform’s operational state at a glance, active agents, workflows, and connectors, along with recent deployments and their status, so an operator can see what is running in production and what is still in staging. Six capabilities cover the agent lifecycle.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent Builder&lt;/strong&gt; is template-driven agent creation, where a team configures an agent’s persona, its tools, and its guardrails rather than wiring those by hand. This is the Golden Path in practice: a new agent starts from a paved template, not a blank repository.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Workflow Designer&lt;/strong&gt; is visual multi-step orchestration with branching, loops, and human-in-the-loop steps, so a multi-step process is composed and reviewed in one place instead of encoded across scattered services.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Connector Registry&lt;/strong&gt; lets a team browse, configure, and publish MCP-compatible data connectors. It’s the operator’s view of the same gateway and tools layer described earlier, which keeps tool publishing governed and reusable across teams.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Live Chat Testing&lt;/strong&gt; provides real-time agent interaction for validation before a production deploy. This is where the evaluation discipline starts. An operator exercises the agent against real inputs before it reaches users, which is the first line of defense against the non-deterministic failures that stall most agent programs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;App Deployment&lt;/strong&gt; covers the full application lifecycle from build to production launch, and &lt;strong&gt;Monitoring Dashboard&lt;/strong&gt; shows agent health, cost, latency, and accuracy metrics in one view, fed by AgentCore Observability and Evaluations. Together they close the loop from shipping an agent to watching how it behaves once it is live.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Underneath the Studio is an embedded software development lifecycle (SDLC) integration we call vibe coding: a team can create an agent through natural language, with automated testing, a preview, and one-click production deployment. The point is the same one that runs through the whole platform: the operator expresses intent and the platform handles the build, the test harness, and the deploy, so the time from idea to a governed production agent is measured in hours.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-2.png" alt="APEX Studio dashboard showing active agents, workflows, and connectors with the six lifecycle capabilities over AgentCore" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: APEX Studio control plane&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The dashboard shows active agents, workflows, and connectors with recent deployment status, and exposes Agent Builder, Workflow Designer, Connector Registry, Live Chat Testing, App Deployment, and a Monitoring Dashboard over AgentCore.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The single integration pattern that does the most work in APEX is the hub-and-spoke model we built on AgentCore Gateway, shown in Figure 3. Without it, every consumer would maintain a bespoke connection to every data service. With N consumers and M services, that is an N by M integration problem: each new application or agent multiplies the number of point-to-point connections we must build, secure, and maintain, and each connection reimplements the same cross-cutting concerns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The hub-and-spoke model avoids that. Every application and agent connects through one gateway instead of holding its own connection to each data service. On the consumer side, an internal analyst, a client chatbot, and a trading agent all speak to the same hub. On the data side, the gateway fans out to Lens Direct for analytics and curves, Short Term Trading for real-time execution, Digital Content for upstream models, and the P&amp;amp;R Dataset calculators. Adding a new consumer or a new data service means wiring one spoke to the hub, not rebuilding the full mesh.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-3.png" alt="Hub-and-spoke diagram of consumers connecting through one AgentCore Gateway that fans out to Lens Direct, Short Term Trading, Digital Content, and P&amp;amp;R Dataset" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Unified MCP Gateway&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consumers (internal analyst, client chatbot, trading agent) connect through a single Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway speaking the MCP protocol, which fans out to data services (Lens Direct, Short Term Trading, Digital Content, P&amp;amp;R Dataset). Cross-cutting concerns are enforced once at the hub.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The gateway speaks the Model Context Protocol (MCP), so it presents a consistent tool interface regardless of what sits behind each spoke. AgentCore Gateway turns existing APIs, AWS Lambda functions, and existing MCP servers into agent-compatible tools, and acts as a single secure endpoint for agents to discover and invoke those tools without custom integration code. It supports IAM, OAuth 2.1, and API key authentication, and AWS partner tools procured through AWS Marketplace can be imported into the gateway directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because traffic funnels through one hub, the cross-cutting concerns are enforced once at the gateway rather than re-implemented in every consumer or spoke: identity, observability, evaluation and guardrails, skills and tools, rate limiting, data protection, and compliance. A policy change applies everywhere the moment it lands at the hub, and every tool call inherits the same identity context, the same telemetry, and the same guardrails. This is also where the MCP server option matters for external access: exposing our data services through a governed MCP endpoint means partners can reach them through a single, controlled interface for direct AI-powered data access rather than through a sprawl of point integrations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="generative-ui-dynamic-components-with-ag-ui-a2ui-and-strands"&gt;Generative UI: Dynamic components with AG-UI, A2UI, and Strands&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A platform is only useful if the agent can surface its work to a user, and rebuilding a streaming, stateful interface for every agent would reintroduce the exact duplication APEX exists to remove. We solve the presentation layer the same way we solved connectivity: with shared protocols. The APEX frontend SDK is built on two complementary protocols, with CopilotKit providing the web components and tooling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AG-UI is the transport. It’s the bi-directional, event-driven runtime connection between an agentic backend and a user-facing application, carrying a stream of typed events such as token streaming, tool calls, and state updates over standard HTTP or Server-Sent Events. It works across stacks, from web to mobile to the command line.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A2UI is the payload. It’s a declarative generative UI specification, originated by Google, that an agent uses to return UI as a JSON blueprint of components rather than text or executable code. The client renders each component from its own catalog of trusted, native widgets, which keeps the UI matched to the host application’s styling and avoids arbitrary code execution. AG-UI carries A2UI blueprints to the frontend, and CopilotKit supports A2UI as a launch partner. In short, A2UI describes what the agent wants to display, AG-UI delivers it, and the client renders it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The agents themselves are Strands agents running in AgentCore runtime. Strands integrates with AG-UI directly, and the combination gives us three capabilities that the ST Trading App, Lens AI, and Woody all reuse rather than rebuild.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Streaming chat.&lt;/strong&gt; AG-UI handles the streaming of messages between users and the agent, so a conversational surface is a configured component rather than a hand-built transport layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool-based generative UI.&lt;/strong&gt; This is where dynamic components come from. The agent does not return only text. AG-UI shares tool information with the client, and the client maps a tool invocation to a rendered component. When an agent calls a tool, the frontend renders the matching component with the tool’s arguments, so a curve-analytics request from Lens Direct or a real-time execution result from Short Term Trading comes back as a live widget rather than a wall of text. For richer cases the agent emits an A2UI blueprint and the client renders it from its trusted component catalog, so the agent can compose an interface, a chart, a form, a result card, without the frontend hardcoding it in advance. A tool the agent already exposes becomes a UI element with a small render binding on the client, which is the composable-building-blocks principle applied to the interface: a single widget or a full workflow, assembled from the same parts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Shared state.&lt;/strong&gt; Strands agents are stateful, and AG-UI synchronizes that state in both directions. The agent sees changes the user makes in the interface, and the interface reflects state the agent updates, so a user editing a parameter and an agent revising a calculation stay in sync without custom wiring.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deployment follows the standard AgentCore path. The Strands agent is configured and launched onto AgentCore runtime, which exposes the &lt;code&gt;POST /invocations&lt;/code&gt; interaction endpoint and a &lt;code&gt;GET /ping&lt;/code&gt; health check. Authentication is handled through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/identity.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/a&gt; using an OAuth authorizer backed by Amazon Cognito, so the same identity model that governs tool calls at the gateway also governs the UI session. The frontend then connects to the deployed runtime endpoint with a bearer token, and the generative UI is live in production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The result is that a team adds a new agent-backed feature by writing agent logic and binding tools and component blueprints, while streaming, state synchronization, identity, and the production runtime all come from the platform.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="ag-ui-in-lens-interactive-ai-on-a-live-dashboard"&gt;AG-UI in Lens: Interactive AI on a live dashboard&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The clearest example is Synapse AI, the generative AI assistant embedded in our Lens application. A user viewing the Lens Power Summary dashboard can open the assistant in a side panel and ask a question in plain language, such as which countries have the most renewable capacity. The agent does not answer from a generic web search. It calls a tool, &lt;code&gt;extractWidgetConfig&lt;/code&gt;, that reads the configuration and data of the ranking widgets already on the dashboard the user is looking at, then returns a ranked table of solar and wind capacity by country rendered inline in the conversation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This is AG-UI doing exactly what it is for. The agent’s reasoning steps and tool calls stream into the panel as typed events as they happen, so the user sees the agent think and then act rather than waiting on a spinner. The result comes back not as a paragraph but as a structured table the agent composed, rendered by the client from its component catalog. And because the assistant reads the dashboard’s own widget state, the response is grounded in the data the user already trusts, not a disconnected query. The interactive assistant is a configured surface on top of a Strands agent, so Lens got a grounded, streaming, in-context analyst without building a transport layer, a state-sync mechanism, or a rendering pipeline of its own.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-4.png" alt="Synapse AI side panel in Lens returning a ranked solar and wind capacity table for the Power Summary dashboard" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Synapse AI inside Lens&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The user asks a question against the Power Summary dashboard. The agent streams its reasoning, calls the &lt;code&gt;extractWidgetConfig&lt;/code&gt; tool to read the dashboard’s ranking widgets, and renders a ranked solar-and-wind capacity table inline in the assistant panel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="agentic-flow-in-woody-orchestrating-tools-into-a-deliverable"&gt;Agentic flow in Woody: Orchestrating tools into a deliverable&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Woody, our internal Wood Mackenzie application, shows the same protocol carrying a longer-running workflow that chains several tools into a finished deliverable. In one flow, an analyst gives Woody a single instruction: research the impact of the Iran conflict on oil prices using both web sources and the Lens Direct dataset, generate charts, and produce a PowerPoint presentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;What follows is the agent planning and then executing across multiple tools. It first reasons about the task and checks which tools it has available, then uses them in sequence: a web search agent tool for open source research, the Lens Direct MCP server for our proprietary data, a Vega-Lite charting tool to generate the visualizations, and a PowerPoint generator to assemble the output. The agent streams its reasoning and its access status as it goes, web research complete, Lens Direct data retrieved, then delivers a research package: four interactive charts covering the oil price trajectory, supply disruption and buffer mechanisms, a price-scenario comparison, and Strait of Hormuz flow, plus a downloadable 15-slide presentation. One natural-language request became a coordinated multi-tool workflow ending in an artifact the analyst can use directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two things in this flow matter for the platform argument. First, the agent mixes a public web tool, a proprietary MCP data connector, a charting tool, and a document generator in a single run, and each of those is an independent building block reached through the gateway. Swapping the charting library, adding a new data connector, or pointing the same workflow at a different model does not change the agent’s logic, because the tools are composed rather than hardcoded. Second, the agent reached our proprietary data through the Lens Direct MCP server, the same connector pattern an external consumer would use, which is the bridge to the next point.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Woody also handles higher-stakes operations with the human in the loop. In a separate flow, an analyst asks Woody to create and train an internal natural-gas demand model. The agent works against the model’s GitHub branch and then pauses at an explicit checkpoint, flagging that a &lt;code&gt;GUIDANCE.md&lt;/code&gt; file needs review and listing the exact fixes required in &lt;code&gt;train.py&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;inference.py&lt;/code&gt; before training can start, and it will not proceed until the analyst confirms. A Task Tracker side panel renders the live run state, the model parameters such as heating and cooling degree days, gas price, storage levels, and renewable generation, alongside the structured results payload, and stays in sync with the agent through AG-UI shared state. This is the Workflow Designer’s human-in-the-loop pattern enforced at runtime: the agent takes no consequential action without approval, which is the control that lets us give it real responsibility over a model pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/ML-21057-5.png" alt="Woody chaining a web search, the Lens Direct MCP server, a charting tool, and a PowerPoint generator, with a live Task Tracker panel" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Agentic workflow in Woody&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;From one instruction, the agent plans across tools, a web search agent, the Lens Direct MCP server, a charting tool, and a PowerPoint generator, then delivers four interactive charts and a fifteen-slide presentation. A separate Woody flow drives model training with a human-in-the-loop checkpoint and a live Task Tracker panel.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="one-platform-internal-and-external-governed-by-entitlements"&gt;One platform, internal and external, governed by entitlements&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Lens and Woody examples run on the same infrastructure. Woody serves internal users, analysts and researchers inside Wood Mackenzie, and Synapse in Lens serves external consumers, while the MCP connectors expose our data to partner systems. All three are the same AgentCore runtime, the same gateway, the same model catalog, and the same observability and guardrails. What differs is not the stack but the access and entitlement controls applied at the hub. Identity is carried through every agent invocation, so the entitlements that govern what an internal analyst can train and deploy are the same mechanism that governs what an external consumer can query and what a partner’s MCP client can reach. We don’t run one platform for employees and a second for customers. We run one platform and let identity-aware authorization decide what each caller sees and does.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;That single foundation is what makes the workflows composable in the normal way teams already work. Because agents are registered in the Woodmac Agent Registry, tools and connectors are published through the gateway, and models are selected from the catalog, a team assembles a workflow by picking the agents, tools, and connectors it needs and snapping them together, the same Lego-block model whether the consumer is an internal researcher or an external client. The Iran-conflict research flow and the model-training flow are built from the same shelf of parts in different combinations. Adding a new connector, swapping a tool, or composing two agents into one workflow is a configuration change in APEX Studio, not a re-platforming exercise, and every combination inherits the identity, guardrails, and tracing enforced at the hub.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="platform-engineering-principles"&gt;Platform engineering principles&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;APEX isn’t only an architecture diagram. It is governed by six principles that determine how we build and operate it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Composable building blocks.&lt;/strong&gt; Every capability is an independent block that works alone or compounds into a larger workflow. A team can take only AgentCore memory, or only the Gateway, or assemble the full set into an autonomous workflow. Pick what you need, snap it together, ship a product.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fuel the flywheel.&lt;/strong&gt; Solving a problem for one team produces a generalized solution for all of them. When we built identity-aware tool access for Lens AI, the ST Trading App inherited it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Abstract the undifferentiated heavy lifting.&lt;/strong&gt; Security, compliance, infrastructure, data, and AI are centralized in the platform so product teams spend their time on what differentiates their product, not on undifferentiated plumbing. The unified MCP Gateway is the clearest example: identity, rate limiting, and compliance are enforced once at the hub, not rebuilt in every consumer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Componentize for reuse.&lt;/strong&gt; Golden paths and reusable SDKs alleviate boilerplate. The frontend SDK and the agent registry are how a new team starts from a paved path instead of a blank page.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Decoupled evolution.&lt;/strong&gt; We keep a strict separation between platform code and business logic. Because AgentCore services are managed and versioned independently, we can upgrade the platform underneath a team without disrupting its agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compounding value.&lt;/strong&gt; More adoption lowers the marginal cost of the next innovation. The platform is a value multiplier: each team that joins makes the next team faster.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="where-this-goes-next-multi-agent-systems"&gt;Where this goes next: Multi-agent systems&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The single agent is the starting point, not the destination. The clear industry direction through 2026 is multi-agent systems, where agents owned by different teams, and eventually different organizations, coordinate to complete work that no single agent can. That shift turns on two open protocols that sit at different layers. MCP is the vertical layer that connects an agent to tools and data, which is what our gateway already standardizes. The Agent-to-Agent (A2A) protocol is the horizontal layer that lets agents discover, delegate to, and collaborate with one another. The two-layer stack, MCP for tools and A2A for agents, is becoming the default architecture for enterprise agent deployments, A2A has moved to Linux Foundation governance with broad vendor backing, and AgentCore runtime already supports A2A.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;APEX is built to grow into this rather than be rebuilt for it. Because every agent is registered in the Woodmac Agent Registry and reached through governed endpoints, a Lens analytics agent, a Short Term Trading execution agent, and a research agent can be composed into a single workflow where one agent delegates a subtask to another, and every hop still carries the same identity context, guardrails, and tracing enforced at the hub. The composable-building-blocks principle that lets us assemble tools into one agent today is the same principle that will let us assemble agents into a team tomorrow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three concrete extensions follow from the architecture already in place. First, agent-to-agent workflows across our own domains, so a trading question can pull a curve from the analytics agent and a calculation from the P&amp;amp;R agent without a human stitching the steps together. Second, governed external collaboration, where the same MCP server endpoint that gives partners direct data access can expose select agents for cross-organization A2A coordination under the entitlements and audit trail we already enforce. Third, deeper autonomy with control, pairing AgentCore Evaluations on live traffic with Policy enforcement on every tool call so that as agents take on longer-running, higher-stakes tasks, the guardrails scale with them. The platform layer absorbs each of these as a new capability, not a rewrite, which is the whole point of building it once.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Most agent programs stall not because the model is inadequate but because the infrastructure around it is missing: no shared runtime, no consistent evaluation, no governed identity, and duplicated plumbing rebuilt for every use case. By standardizing on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, we turned three parallel agent-building efforts into one shared platform. A team can now build an agent in hours rather than months, swap models without touching business logic, and ship to production with guardrails, tracing, identity, and evaluation in place from the first invocation. The platform layer is managed and model-agnostic, scales automatically, and bills only for the resources each agent actually consumes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The result is that our teams spend their time on the business logic that differentiates the energy products our customers use, and not on rebuilding authentication, memory, and observability for the fourth time. As more teams adopt APEX the marginal cost of the next agent keeps falling, and because the platform is built on the open MCP and A2A protocols, the same foundation that runs single agents today extends to the multi-agent systems coming next. To learn more, explore the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; documentation, review &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore pricing&lt;/a&gt;, or leave a comment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>How MRH Trowe enabled secure self-service AI agents in financial services</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-mrh-trowe-enabled-secure-self-service-ai-agents-in-financial-services/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Marouane El Bostahi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 17 Sep 2026 15:36:42 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">3897ac24762f2462158b7a05b94713494788aa8c</guid>

					<description>Learn how MRH Trowe, one of Germany's leading commercial and industrial insurance brokers, gave about 400 employees secure, self-service access to AI agents in its first month of production - using Strands Agents, Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and LibreChat to meet the security, data residency, and compliance requirements of the German financial sector.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Basic AI chat isn’t enough for financial services organizations that need secure, self-service AI agents. In financial services, employees need AI that can work with internal systems and sensitive client data, stay inside a governed environment, and remain auditable and cost-transparent. All of this must happen without every team standing up its own tools.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how &lt;a href="https://www.mrh-trowe.com/en/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MRH Trowe&lt;/a&gt;, one of Germany’s leading commercial and industrial insurance brokers, gave approximately 400 employees secure access to self-service AI agents. The rollout reached this scale in the first month of production. The framework combines &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://www.librechat.ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;LibreChat&lt;/a&gt; to deliver the security, data residency, and compliance controls required in the German financial sector. The initial production cost was approximately $14 per seat in the first month, with a clear path to reduce infrastructure costs by about 40 percent through right-sizing and scheduled scaling.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="about-mrh-trowe"&gt;About MRH Trowe&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MRH Trowe is an owner-managed commercial and industrial insurance broker operating primarily in Germany, Switzerland, and Austria. Growing through both organic expansion and acquisitions, MRH Trowe was among the first German insurance brokers to operate on an exclusively cloud-based IT infrastructure. AWS is the preferred partner for this strategy, and the use of AI is a strategic corporate goal, specifically to streamline internal processes and enhance efficiency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-challenge-self-service-ai-without-losing-control"&gt;The challenge: Self-service AI without losing control&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As a commercial insurance broker in a highly regulated industry, MRH Trowe saw growing demand from employees across the business to use generative AI in their daily work. Individual teams were beginning to experiment with AI on their own, which created the risk of fragmented, unmanaged tools and potential exposure of sensitive client and insurance data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MRH Trowe needed a way to let employees build and use AI agents themselves, quickly and without deep technical skills. At the same time, everything had to stay within a secure, centrally governed environment that meets the data protection and compliance requirements of the financial sector. The core tension was clear: balance broad, self-service access to AI with the security, control, and cost transparency the organization required. The MRH Trowe vision: “every question should be first answered by AI before any human intervention, and repetitive processes should be automated by those who did them in the past.”&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Basic chat interfaces fell short on several capabilities the business needed:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Contextual responses&lt;/strong&gt; – Grounded in internal data, not only generic model knowledge.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-step workflows&lt;/strong&gt; – Tasks that require gathering information, reasoning over it, and taking action across several steps.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Institutional data integration&lt;/strong&gt; – Secure connections to internal systems and document repositories.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Centralized governance&lt;/strong&gt; – A single, governed application rather than shadow AI tools spread across teams.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="the-building-blocks"&gt;The building blocks&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution brings together three technologies, each addressing a specific part of the challenge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="strands-agents"&gt;Strands Agents&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MRH Trowe’s builders use Strands Agents, an open source SDK that creates agents in a few lines of code. Strands handles the agent patterns, such as orchestration and reasoning, and scales from a builder’s laptop to production, so teams focus on the use case rather than the infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-bedrock-agentcore"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To run agents in production, MRH Trowe uses Amazon Bedrock AgentCore as a platform to build, connect, and optimize agents at scale, with any framework or model. AgentCore was the deciding factor for MRH Trowe: it natively supports open source frameworks such as Strands Agents, isolates each agent session at the compute and filesystem level, and offers a consumption-based model that gives the team cost transparency as adoption grows. This means the team keeps the flexibility of Strands without giving up the security and session isolation as a regulated broker requires.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="librechat"&gt;LibreChat&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;LibreChat is an open source alternative to commercial AI chat interfaces. It gives MRH Trowe a familiar, brandable chat experience that drives immediate adoption, with the controls a regulated organization needs:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;User management&lt;/strong&gt; – Robust authentication and authorization to manage access across the employee base with appropriate permissions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Token budgets&lt;/strong&gt; – LibreChat’s built-in token budget system avoids unexpected costs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Multi-model support&lt;/strong&gt; – Flexibility to choose the most appropriate models per use case while avoiding vendor lock-in.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Conversation management&lt;/strong&gt; – Employees can organize their AI interactions, creating a structured environment.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customizable interface&lt;/strong&gt; – Branded to match MRH Trowe’s identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="in-practice-from-a-microsoft-teams-meeting-to-a-structured-protocol-in-minutes"&gt;In practice: From a Microsoft Teams meeting to a structured protocol in minutes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The first agent MRH Trowe put into production turns a Microsoft Teams meeting into ready-to-use meeting minutes. In LibreChat, an employee asks, in German, for a recent meeting with a given participant. The agent finds the meeting on the employee’s calendar, retrieves the transcript, and drafts a structured meeting summary with date, participants, agenda, topics, and action items. Manual note-taking after every call turns into a one-line request.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two design choices make this safe for a regulated broker. First, every request runs as the signed-in employee. LibreChat authenticates through Microsoft Entra ID and passes the user’s identity to the agent server-side, so an agent can only reach that employee’s own calendar and transcript. The identity can’t be set from the chat box. Second, processing stays in-Region: the agents, model, and data run in the AWS Europe (Frankfurt) AWS Region, keeping client and meeting data in Germany. Amazon Bedrock AgentCore and the foundation models used in this solution are available in the Europe (Frankfurt) Region (eu-central-1). Service and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-region-compatibility.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model availability&lt;/a&gt; varies by AWS Region.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-architecture"&gt;Solution architecture&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution is deployed in a single AWS account inside a virtual private cloud (VPC), with employees connecting from the corporate network. The following diagram shows the solution architecture, including the connectivity, application, data, and agent layers described in this section.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21177-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21177-1.png" alt="Solution architecture showing the connectivity, application, data, and agent layers in a single AWS account" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture spanning the connectivity, application, data, and agent layers&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The main components are:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Secure connectivity&lt;/strong&gt; – Employees access the system from anywhere using a transit gateway implementation combined with a zero-trust provider, keeping traffic on a private, dedicated connection rather than the public internet. An internal Application Load Balancer (ALB) routes requests to the application tier. &lt;p&gt;The following network view expands the secure-connectivity path, showing how on-premises users connect through the AWS networking account to the internal load balancer in the application account’s private subnet.&lt;/p&gt; &lt;p&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;div style="width: 528px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
   &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21177-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21177-2.png" alt="Network diagram showing on-premises users connecting through the AWS networking account to an internal load balancer in a private subnet" width="518"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
   &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Network path from on-premises users through the AWS networking account to the internal load balancer&lt;/p&gt;
  &lt;/div&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Application tier (LibreChat)&lt;/strong&gt; – The chat experience runs as containerized services on Amazon Elastic Container Service (Amazon ECS) with AWS Fargate as the launch type, including the LibreChat service, a Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) API service, and a MeiliSearch service for fast text search without vectorizing the data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data layer&lt;/strong&gt;. 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon DocumentDB&lt;/strong&gt; (with MongoDB compatibility) for users, sessions, and conversation data.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon ElastiCache&lt;/strong&gt; for caching and session state.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) for PostgreSQL&lt;/strong&gt; as the relational and vector store backing LibreChat’s RAG API for uploaded documents.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon OpenSearch Service&lt;/strong&gt; as the vector store for Amazon Bedrock, supporting retrieval over content ingested from Confluence.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Elastic File System (Amazon EFS)&lt;/strong&gt; for MeiliSearch indexes.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/strong&gt; for uploaded files and chat artifacts.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Agent layer (Strands + AgentCore)&lt;/strong&gt; – Strands Agents are hosted on AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and exposed to LibreChat through custom endpoints backed by AWS Lambda and Amazon API Gateway. With this pattern, the team can load new agents in LibreChat without updates or downtime to the chat application and roll out new agentic applications independently as adoption grows.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the usage dashboard MRH Trowe built to track adoption and cost, including unique users, token consumption by model, and cost per user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21177-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-21177-3.png" alt="Usage dashboard showing unique users, token consumption by model, unique chats, cost per user, and total AWS costs" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Usage dashboard tracking unique users, token consumption by model, unique chats, and cost per user&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-a-request-flows"&gt;How a request flows&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following steps describe how a typical request travels through the system, from the employee’s browser to the agent and back.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An employee opens LibreChat and selects an agent from a switch panel.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;LibreChat authenticates the user through MRH Trowe’s existing identity provider, Microsoft Entra ID. Access control lists (ACLs) in the LibreChat admin panel determine which endpoints and models the user can access.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;When the user invokes an agent, LibreChat calls a custom endpoint through API Gateway, secured with an API key. Or if the endpoint supports On-behalf-of (OBO) authentication flow, this can also be used for user authentication.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Lambda function uses an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role to invoke the Strands agent running on AgentCore runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The agent reasons over the request, calls its tools and authorized data sources, and returns the response through LibreChat, within an isolated, governed session.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-and-compliance-for-a-regulated-environment"&gt;Security and compliance for a regulated environment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Operating in the German financial sector means data protection and governance are not optional. The architecture applies a layered approach:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Data residency in Germany&lt;/strong&gt; – Sensitive client and insurance data stays within the chosen AWS Region.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Private connectivity&lt;/strong&gt; – A transit gateway setup combined with zero trust keeps employee traffic off the public internet.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Session isolation&lt;/strong&gt; – AgentCore runtime isolates each agent session at the compute and filesystem level.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Central governance&lt;/strong&gt; – A single, managed environment replaces fragmented, unmanaged tools, giving the organization visibility and control over how AI is used.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Strong authentication and authorization mechanism&lt;/strong&gt; – Active Directory group-based roles and access for single sign-on (SSO) and zero-trust solution.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With this foundation, MRH Trowe can serve employees in a secure and highly flexible environment. AI is now at the fingertips of every employee, and exploration is encouraged, while the AWS implementation keeps security and governance at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results-and-business-impact"&gt;Results and business impact&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution ran at an infrastructure-and-token cost of about $14 per seat per month.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The environment has also become a factory for new use cases. The most recent is “talk to your data”, which supports business-led analyses such as cross-sell and upsell reviews that combine CRM data with publicly available information.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To drive adoption, MRH Trowe paired the rollout with organizational change:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run use case workshops across teams to create and maintain a power user group.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Use adoption data to identify power users and promote their workflows across the organization.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“If you and your colleague are doing things twice, consider creating a LibreChat agent!”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;— Leonid Karlinsky, Board member, MRH Trowe&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond cost efficiency, the framework delivered a secure, self-service way for employees to build and use AI agents without deep technical skills, all within a centrally governed environment that meets the compliance requirements of a regulated financial services organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;MRH Trowe shows that a regulated financial services organization can have both broad, self-service access to AI and the control its industry demands. By combining the flexibility of open source (Strands Agents and LibreChat) with the security, scalability, and reliability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, MRH Trowe gave roughly 400 employees a centrally governed way to build and use AI agents in their first month with a clear path to make it even more economical as adoption grows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By shaping a Data and AI Community of Practice, MRH Trowe aims for 10–15 agents created and maintained by subject matter experts by the end of 2026.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about building AI agents on AWS, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore detail page&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore documentation&lt;/a&gt;. To get started with open source agent development, see the &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt;
