		<item>
		<title>Fault tolerant distributed training on Amazon EKS using NVRx</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fault-tolerant-distributed-training-on-amazon-eks-using-nvrx/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aravind Neelakantan]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 16 Sep 2026 18:59:25 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">1e1539eea3be4ce999a3a6472187f690d96aec84</guid>

					<description>Integrate NVIDIA Resiliency Extension (NVRx) into PyTorch FSDP training on Amazon EKS to overlap checkpoint I/O with training and recover from GPU faults in seconds. This post covers async checkpointing, in-process restart, and ft_launcher in-job restart, with H100 benchmarks at 2 to 8 nodes showing 99%+ training efficiency and second-scale recovery.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Large-scale distributed training jobs run for hours or days across dozens of nodes. At that scale and duration, interruptions are statistically inevitable: network partitions, memory errors, software exceptions, or infrastructure events will eventually disrupt at least one worker. A single GPU fault triggers a cascade: NVIDIA Collective Communication Library (NCCL) timeouts propagate to healthy workers, pods crash and restart out of sync, and your cluster burns expensive GPU hours while making zero training progress. Synchronous checkpointing adds a second source of idle time: every save blocks all ranks on I/O, which on the cluster sizes in this post consumed up to 40% of total wall time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we show how to integrate NVIDIA Resiliency Extension (NVRx) into PyTorch Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP) training on Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS) to solve both problems. You walk through async checkpointing that overlaps I/O with training, in-process restart that recovers from faults in seconds without touching the container lifecycle, and in-job restart using &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; for automatic worker respawn on hard crashes. We include benchmark results on H100 GPUs at 2-node to 8-node scale, with all code available to reproduce.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution combines NVRx fault tolerance primitives with an EKS-based training environment designed for high-performance multi-node GPU workloads. NVRx handles the application-level resilience (async checkpointing, in-process restart, and in-job restart), while the EKS cluster provides the infrastructure foundation: GPU scheduling, high-bandwidth networking, and shared storage for checkpoint persistence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="nvrx"&gt;NVRx&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The NVIDIA Resiliency Extension (NVRx) is a pip-installable Python layer (&lt;code&gt;pip install nvidia-resiliency-ext&lt;/code&gt;) that adds fault-tolerance primitives to PyTorch: no custom kernels, no PyTorch fork, no recompile. The primitives drop into an existing FSDP script as ordinary imports. The model and training code stay untouched. Each is independently adoptable. We exercised three features: async checkpointing, in-process restart, and &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; (in-job restart).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Async checkpointing&lt;/strong&gt;, exposed through &lt;code&gt;TorchAsyncCheckpoint&lt;/code&gt;, replaces &lt;code&gt;torch.save&lt;/code&gt; with an &lt;code&gt;async_save()&lt;/code&gt; call that hands the state dict to a background process and returns immediately. A matching &lt;code&gt;finalize_async_save()&lt;/code&gt; before the next save commits the prior write. Paired with FSDP &lt;code&gt;LOCAL_STATE_DICT&lt;/code&gt;, each rank writes its own shard directly, with no all-gather and no rank-0 bottleneck.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;In-process restart&lt;/strong&gt;, exposed through &lt;code&gt;inprocess.Wrapper&lt;/code&gt;, wraps the train function so a transient fault (an unhandled exception or an NCCL hang) does not kill the Python process. NVRx aborts the active process group, runs health checks per rank (GPU, NVLink, NIC), re-rendezvouses survivors, and re-enters the wrapped function from the latest checkpoint. The interpreter, CUDA allocator, and outer-scope objects survive. This catches the soft-fault class.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; binary&lt;/strong&gt;, the NVRx in-job restart launcher, handles cases in-process cannot catch: SIGKILL, out-of-memory (OOM) kill, and OS-level hangs. Each rank runs a &lt;code&gt;RankMonitorClient&lt;/code&gt;. The launcher checks heartbeats against explicit CLI-set timeouts, and on stall or death it kills survivors, reclaims GPU memory, and respawns fresh workers in the same job. Recovered workers reload from the latest checkpoint. Each recovery layer covers a distinct fault class: in-process for soft faults, &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; for hard faults, and the cluster orchestrator for node loss. The layers are independent. Pick the one whose scope matches your failure modes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-eks-cluster"&gt;Amazon EKS cluster&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon EKS is a managed Kubernetes service that handles the control plane, upgrades, and API server availability. We run self-managed node groups of p5.48xlarge instances, each with 8 NVIDIA H100 80 GB GPUs and 32 Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) network interfaces. Training pods run as Kubernetes Jobs with headless Services for peer discovery, so workers find each other through DNS rather than hardcoded IPs, and pod replacements can rejoin without reconfiguring the job.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each node exposes its GPUs and EFA adapters as extended resources through the NVIDIA device plugin and EFA device plugin. The Kubernetes scheduler places training pods on GPU nodes using node affinity and tolerations, facilitating the full 8-GPU allocation per node.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For checkpoint storage, we use Amazon FSx for Lustre (SCRATCH_2, 1.2 TB) mounted into every training pod through the FSx CSI driver. FSx provides the shared filesystem that both async and synchronous checkpointing write to, and critically, it is where recovering workers read their checkpoint state after a fault. Placing FSx in the same Availability Zone as the GPU nodes minimizes read latency during recovery, which matters because checkpoint loading (not the restart mechanism) dominates recovery time at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_139421" style="width: 1534px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-139421" loading="lazy" class="wp-image-139421 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/Screenshot-2026-09-15-at-11.12.04 AM.png" alt="" width="1524" height="984"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-139421" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Architecture diagram showing the EKS cluster with 2-8 p5 nodes, EFA interconnect, FSx for Lustre, and NVRx components within training pods&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The key AWS services involved:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon EKS — Kubernetes control plane, pod scheduling, Job lifecycle management.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) p5.48xlarge — 8x H100 80 GB GPUs, 32x EFA adapters per node.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Elastic Fabric Adapter (EFA) — 3,200 Gbps network bandwidth for NCCL all-reduce operations.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon FSx for Lustre — Shared POSIX filesystem for distributed checkpoint I/O.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) — Container registry for the training image (PyTorch + NVRx + model code)&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before deploying this solution, make sure you have the following infrastructure and tooling in place:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS account with service quota for p5.48xlarge (or p4de.24xlarge) instances.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon EKS cluster (v1.28+) with EFA-enabled self-managed GPU node groups and the NVIDIA device plugin installed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon FSx for Lustre filesystem (SCRATCH_2) in the same Availability Zone as the GPU nodes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Container image with PyTorch 2.9+, NVRx 0.4.1 to reproduce the benchmark results shown in this post. Use v0.6.0 with an updated launcher configuration for a current deployment and your training code, pushed to Amazon ECR.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;kubectl configured for your cluster.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;HuggingFace account with access to &lt;code&gt;meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B&lt;/code&gt; (or your model of choice)&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Training dataset pre-downloaded to shared storage (we use 100K samples from the C4 dataset. You can use a dataset of your choice)&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For EKS cluster creation with GPU nodes and EFA networking, see the infrastructure guides in &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai/tree/main/1.architectures" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;awsome-distributed-ai/1.architectures&lt;/a&gt;. For the complete NVRx-specific setup including Terraform modules, container build, and dataset preparation, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai/tree/main/examples/training/nvrx" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;NVRx test case README&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-walkthrough"&gt;Solution walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVRx exposes two orthogonal changes to a standard PyTorch FSDP script: async checkpointing (a write-path optimization) and recovery, which comes in two independent layers: in-process restart for soft faults and &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; in-job restart for hard faults. The test case ships a separate script per capability, so you adopt only what you need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We start from a baseline FSDP script with synchronous checkpointing, then introduce each NVRx capability independently (async checkpointing, in-process restart, and the &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; in-job restart) and close with a short note on how the recovery layers compose.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="baseline-fsdp-training-script"&gt;Baseline FSDP training script&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Start with a minimal FSDP loop launched by &lt;code&gt;torchrun&lt;/code&gt; using a synchronous distributed checkpoint save (PyTorch’s &lt;code&gt;torch.distributed.checkpoint.save&lt;/code&gt; under the hood). The save blocks every rank until per-rank shards land on shared storage, and a worker crash kills the entire training job, requiring a full restart from the last checkpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Baseline FSDP training --- torchrun launches; sync checkpoints block the loop.
rank = int(os.environ["RANK"])
world_size = int(os.environ["WORLD_SIZE"])
local_rank = int(os.environ["LOCAL_RANK"])
torch.cuda.set_device(local_rank)
dist.init_process_group(backend="nccl")

model, _ = create_model(args.model_name, args.torch_dtype)
model = wrap_model(model, "fsdp", local_rank, args.model_name)
optimizer = torch.optim.AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=args.learning_rate)
data_iter = iter(create_dataloader(args, tokenizer, rank, world_size))

for step in range(1, args.max_steps + 1):
    loss = train_step(model, next(data_iter), optimizer)  # fwd / bwd / step

    if step % args.checkpoint_interval == 0:
        # save_checkpoint() uses dcp.save() under the hood for FSDP --- collective.
        # All ranks block here until per-rank shards land on shared storage.
        save_checkpoint(model, optimizer, step,
                        args.checkpoint_path, rank, "fsdp")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="async-checkpointing-decouple-checkpoint-io-from-training"&gt;Async checkpointing: decouple checkpoint I/O from training&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-20900-2-1.png" alt="Diagram of the NVRx async checkpoint pipeline, showing the main training thread handing the state dict to a background write process" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: NVRx async checkpoint pipeline&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Replace the synchronous save with the NVRx &lt;code&gt;TorchAsyncCheckpoint&lt;/code&gt;: instantiate it once with &lt;code&gt;persistent_queue=True&lt;/code&gt;, call &lt;code&gt;async_save(state_dict, path)&lt;/code&gt; in place of &lt;code&gt;torch.save&lt;/code&gt;, and call &lt;code&gt;finalize_async_save(blocking=True)&lt;/code&gt; once as a blocking finalization at job exit, as shown in figure 2. A background process owns the actual write. The main thread continues to the next forward/backward step. Each rank writes its own shard through FSDP &lt;code&gt;LOCAL_STATE_DICT&lt;/code&gt;, with no all-gather and no rank-0 bottleneck.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import torch
import torch.distributed as dist
from nvidia_resiliency_ext.checkpointing.async_ckpt.torch_ckpt import TorchAsyncCheckpoint

# Initialize the async checkpoint manager once after model/optimizer setup.
async_ckpt = TorchAsyncCheckpoint(persistent_queue=True)

# Training loop
for step, batch in enumerate(dataloader):
    loss = model(batch)
    loss.backward()
    optimizer.step()

    # Checkpoint every N steps
    if step % checkpoint_interval == 0:
        state_dict = build_state_dict(model, optimizer, step)  # FSDP LOCAL_STATE_DICT, CPU-staged
        # torch.save(state_dict, path)  # was blocking
        async_ckpt.async_save(state_dict, path)  # returns immediately

# Drain any in-flight save at job exit.
async_ckpt.finalize_async_save(blocking=True)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="in-process-restart"&gt;In-process restart&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Wrap the train function with &lt;code&gt;inprocess.Wrapper&lt;/code&gt;. The wrapper owns the restart loop: an exception in the wrapped function (or a hang the watchdog detects) triggers a re-entry instead of crashing the process. The constructor wires four concerns: timeouts (soft_timeout, hard_timeout, barrier_timeout, completion_timeout), health checks (CudaHealthCheck + FaultCounter), a RetryController that caps the total number of restarts and sets a minimum surviving world size, and rank assignment (ActivateAllRanks + ShiftRanks) that pulls survivors left to keep the world contiguous.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-20900-3-1.png" alt="Diagram of the NVRx in-process restart architecture, showing the wrapper, watchdog, health checks, and rank reassignment" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: NVRx in-process restart architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import nvidia_resiliency_ext.inprocess as inprocess
from nvidia_resiliency_ext.inprocess import CallWrapper

def train_with_inprocess_restart(args, restart_metrics, base_store=None,
                                 call_wrapper: CallWrapper = None):
    # Re-init dist, rebuild FSDP, load latest checkpoint, train.
    # call_wrapper.ping() each step;
    # call_wrapper.iteration tells you which restart you're on.
    ...

wrapped_train = inprocess.Wrapper(
    store_kwargs = {"host_name": master_addr, "port": master_port + 1},
    soft_timeout = datetime.timedelta(seconds=args.soft_timeout_seconds),
    hard_timeout = datetime.timedelta(seconds=args.hard_timeout_seconds),
    barrier_timeout = datetime.timedelta(seconds=args.barrier_timeout_seconds),
    completion_timeout = datetime.timedelta(seconds=args.barrier_timeout_seconds),
    health_check = inprocess.Compose(
        inprocess.health_check.CudaHealthCheck(),
        inprocess.health_check.FaultCounter(max_rank_faults=20)),
    initialize = inprocess.initialize.RetryController(
        max_iterations=args.max_restarts, min_active_world_size=1),
    rank_assignment = inprocess.Compose(
        inprocess.rank_assignment.ActivateAllRanks(),
        inprocess.rank_assignment.ShiftRanks()),
)(train_with_inprocess_restart)

wrapped_train(args, restart_metrics, base_store)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Behind the scenes (as shown in figure 3): a &lt;code&gt;ProgressWatchdog&lt;/code&gt; (&lt;code&gt;progress_watchdog.py&lt;/code&gt;:49) uses &lt;code&gt;Py_AddPendingCall&lt;/code&gt; to write timestamps between bytecode instructions, so a hang inside NCCL (a C extension) is still detectable. A MonitorThread (&lt;code&gt;monitor_thread.py&lt;/code&gt;:124) polls the inprocess TCPStore for an interrupted flag and raises RankShouldRestart into the main thread on detection. AbortTorchDistributed (&lt;code&gt;abort.py&lt;/code&gt;:62) then collects Flight Recorder traces, aborts NCCL backends, and destroys the process group. Health checks pick survivors. The Python process stays alive, and only the distributed process group is rebuilt.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="in-job-restart-ft_launcher"&gt;In-job restart (ft_launcher)&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Switch the launcher from &lt;code&gt;torchrun&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt;. It understands the same rendezvous flags and adds a few of its own to control restart behavior:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;# --- before ---
torchrun --nnodes=$NNODES --nproc_per_node=$GPU_PER_NODE \
    --rdzv-backend=c10d --rdzv-endpoint=$MASTER_ADDR:29500 \
    train.py [args]

# --- after ---
ft_launcher --nnodes=$NNODES --nproc_per_node=$GPU_PER_NODE \
    --rdzv_backend=c10d --rdzv_endpoint=$MASTER_ADDR:29500 \
    --max-restarts=20 --ft-restart-policy=any-failed \
    --ft-rank-heartbeat-timeout=900 \
    --ft-initial-rank-heartbeat-timeout=1200 \
    --monitor-interval=5 \
    train_ft_launcher.py [args]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Inside the train script, instantiate RankMonitorClient once after distributed init and send a heartbeat each step:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;import nvidia_resiliency_ext.fault_tolerance as fault_tolerance

ft_client = fault_tolerance.RankMonitorClient()
ft_client.init_workload_monitoring()  # once, after dist init

for step in range(1, args.max_steps + 1):
    loss = train_step(model, next(data_iter), optimizer)
    ft_client.send_heartbeat()  # liveness signal to RankMonitorServer&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Set &lt;code&gt;--ft-rank-heartbeat-timeout&lt;/code&gt; above the longest legitimate interval between application heartbeats. The example uses 900 seconds. Setting &lt;code&gt;--ft-initial-rank-heartbeat-timeout=1200&lt;/code&gt; (20 minutes) accommodates first-time model loading. See &lt;code&gt;kubernetes/training-job-ft-launcher.yaml&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-20900-4-1.png" alt="Diagram of the NVRx in-job restart (ft_launcher) architecture, showing RankMonitorServer heartbeat tracking and fresh worker respawn" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: NVRx in-job restart (ft_launcher) architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Behind the scenes (as shown in figure 4): a &lt;code&gt;RankMonitorServer&lt;/code&gt; per rank tracks heartbeat intervals against the timeouts above. On timeout, the launcher SIGTERMs survivors (SIGKILL for stragglers), reclaims GPU memory, re-rendezvouses, and spawns fresh workers. Workers reload from the latest checkpoint on startup. Checkpoint frequency caps the amount of lost work.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="how-the-layers-cover-distinct-fault-classes"&gt;How the layers cover distinct fault classes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The two recovery layers are scoped to different failure modes: in-process catches what fits inside one Python process (transient exceptions, watchdog-visible NCCL hangs), &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; catches what kills the process or hangs at the OS level (SIGKILL, OOM, sub-Python deadlocks), and the cluster scheduler catches node loss. Pick the layer whose blast radius matches your failure modes. Async checkpointing is orthogonal: it pairs with a recovery layer (or none) and caps the lost-work blast radius.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="deploy-and-run-experiments"&gt;Deploy and run experiments&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We deploy training jobs using a thin wrapper script around &lt;code&gt;kubectl&lt;/code&gt; that handles manifest templating, job cleanup, and environment variable substitution. Environment variables define the instance type, GPU count, number of EFA devices, and other hardware-specific parameters, so the same training code and manifests run on different GPU types (p5, p4de) by changing a single configuration file. To compare recovery mechanisms under identical conditions, we use deterministic fault injection, pre-generating exactly N faults at fixed training steps and ranks using a seeded RNG:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;--fault_count=5 --fault_seed=42 --fault_types=exception,hang --fault_type_weights=0.6,0.4&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same seed produces the same fault schedule across experiments, enabling direct comparison between baseline K8s restart, ft_launcher, and NVRx in-process restart. We run each mechanism against the same 5-fault pattern, then run async vs sync checkpointing separately without fault injection to isolate checkpoint overhead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section presents benchmarking results for two key capabilities: async checkpointing and fault recovery.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="async-checkpointing"&gt;Async checkpointing&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We benchmarked async (NVRx) vs synchronous (&lt;code&gt;torch.save&lt;/code&gt;) checkpointing on LLaMA-3.1-8B FSDP training across two dimensions: scaling from 2 to 8 nodes (16-64 H100 GPUs) with a fixed checkpoint interval of every 1000 steps, and sweeping checkpoint frequency (every 100 to 1000 steps) at 8-node scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-20900-5-1.png" alt="Chart comparing synchronous and async checkpointing training efficiency from 2 to 8 nodes" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Sync vs async checkpointing&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Async checkpointing maintains 99%+ training efficiency at every scale (99.2% at 2 nodes, 99.8% at 8 nodes), while synchronous checkpointing stays flat at 57-61%, losing ~40% of wall time to blocking I/O regardless of node count. The gap persists because checkpoint overhead is storage-bound: FSx Lustre write time (~275s) is constant whether you have 16 or 64 GPUs. Async hides this latency entirely by overlapping I/O with the next training segment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When we sweep checkpoint frequency at 8-node scale, the async advantage becomes more pronounced:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-20900-6-1.png" alt="Chart showing the impact of checkpointing frequency on efficiency for sync compared to async at 8-node scale" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Checkpointing frequency impact&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At every-1000-steps, async achieves 99.8% efficiency vs 60.3% sync. At every-100-steps, sync plummets to 14.7% while async degrades gracefully to 29.6%, still 2x more efficient. The crossover point is where the training gap between checkpoints (~280s at 100 steps) approaches the FSx I/O time (~275s). Above that threshold, async fully overlaps. Below it, partial overlap still outperforms blocking writes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What this means&lt;/strong&gt;: Teams training at scale often checkpoint infrequently to avoid the blocking penalty, accepting hours of lost progress when faults occur. Async checkpointing removes that tradeoff: you can checkpoint aggressively (every few minutes) with near-zero overhead, minimizing rollback distance without sacrificing GPU utilization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="fault-recovery"&gt;Fault recovery&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We ran fault recovery experiments on 2 p5.48xlarge nodes (16 H100 GPUs total) training LLaMA-3.1-8B with FSDP, checkpointing every 500 steps over a 2000-step run. We injected 5 deterministic faults into each training run (same seed, same fault schedule) and compared three recovery mechanisms:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="images/image7.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/15/ML-20900-7.png" alt="Chart comparing fault recovery methods across training and infrastructure goodput: in-process restart, in-job restart, and Kubernetes restart" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 7: Comparing fault recovery methods: in-process restart vs in-job restart vs Kubernetes restart&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;NVRx in-process restart achieves 31% training goodput with 87% infrastructure goodput, recovering in ~10 seconds with zero container restarts. &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; achieves 25.5% training goodput (85.9% infra goodput) with 17s recovery per fault. Baseline Kubernetes restart is catastrophic: 11.5% training goodput, 35.8% infra goodput, and 270s recovery per fault. The baseline failure mode is a cascade: one rank crashes, the surviving rank hits an NCCL timeout (60s), then both pods restart out of sync, triggering CrashLoopBackOff and additional timeout cycles before training can resume.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What this means&lt;/strong&gt;: At large-scale training (hundreds to thousands of GPUs), hardware faults occur every few hours. With baseline Kubernetes recovery consuming 4+ minutes per fault, a cluster experiencing 3-4 faults per hour would spend more time recovering than training. NVRx reduces that recovery window to seconds, keeping GPU utilization high even as fault frequency increases with scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, remove the resources you created. Order matters: Kubernetes objects that mount FSx must go before the filesystem itself, or deletion blocks on volume finalizers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete training jobs. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;./deploy.sh --delete training-job-inprocess.yaml
kubectl get jobs -n nvrx-training&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Release the volume claim. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;kubectl delete -f fsx-storage.yaml&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;The PersistentVolume uses persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain, so this detaches the filesystem without deleting it, and FSx keeps billing until you remove it in step 4. Copy any results you want to keep off /checkpoints first. Deleting a SCRATCH_2 filesystem is permanent and takes no final backup.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Scale GPU node groups to zero. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws autoscaling update-auto-scaling-group \
    --auto-scaling-group-name &amp;lt;your-gpu-asg-name&amp;gt; \
    --min-size 0 --max-size 0 --desired-capacity 0&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;On-demand instances stop billing once terminated. With EC2 Capacity Blocks the reservation fee is charged up front, so releasing early recovers nothing, but scale down at least 30 minutes before the block ends, since EC2 begins terminating Capacity Block instances ahead of the end time.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Delete the filesystem, image repository, and cluster. 
  &lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
   &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws fsx delete-file-system --file-system-id &amp;lt;your-fsx-id&amp;gt;
aws ecr delete-repository --repository-name &amp;lt;your-repo-name&amp;gt; --force&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
  &lt;/div&gt; &lt;p&gt;Then remove the EKS cluster and GPU node groups using whichever tool provisioned them (terraform destroy, eksctl delete cluster, or CloudFormation), so infrastructure state stays consistent.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For job-level teardown in context, see Stop Training (https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai/tree/main/examples/training/nvrx/kubernetes#8-stop-training) in the test case README.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we demonstrated how combining NVIDIA Resiliency Extension (NVRx) with Amazon EKS unlocks more efficient distributed PyTorch training by alleviating two common bottlenecks: checkpoint blocking and fault recovery.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the reported runs, async checkpointing reduced blocking I/O and was measured at 99%+ training efficiency from 16 to 64 H100 GPUs. For fault recovery, NVRx in-process restart can recover in ~10 seconds with zero container restarts, while &lt;code&gt;ft_launcher&lt;/code&gt; handles hard faults (SIGKILL, hangs) that crash the process entirely. Both represent a step change from baseline Kubernetes recovery, where NCCL timeout cascades and CrashLoopBackOff storms consume 4+ minutes per fault.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The combination of these features means teams can checkpoint aggressively and recover quickly, maximizing both data protection and GPU utilization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="next-steps"&gt;Next steps&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Try the full reproducible example in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/awsome-distributed-ai/tree/main/examples/training/nvrx" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;awsome-distributed-ai repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Explore combining async checkpointing with fault recovery for maximum training efficiency, reducing both rollback distance and recovery time simultaneously.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Optimizing agent system prompts with Amazon Bedrock AgentCore</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/optimizing-agent-system-prompts-with-amazon-bedrock-agentcore/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Han Ding]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 16 Sep 2026 15:47:39 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Best Practices]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">4fc315fa9f77dfa231ce4ad1a494c418d1a77b51</guid>

					<description>AgentCore optimization turns production traces into proposed configuration changes, then validates them before promotion. This technical companion to the launch post explains how the system prompt optimizer's reflector engine works and shares benchmark results for the Single Agent and Sub-Agent Reflectors.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;In a &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-agent-quality-optimization-in-agentcore-now-in-preview/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;previous launch post&lt;/a&gt;, we introduced AgentCore optimization, a capability of &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/optimization.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; that can help you improve the quality of your agents. Improving a low-scoring agent has traditionally been a manual process. You review long traces to find where the agent goes wrong, tune individual components such as prompts, tool descriptions, and skills, and rerun evaluations to check for improvement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With AgentCore optimization, you can use production traces to propose configuration changes, validate them through offline batch evaluation and online A/B testing on live traffic, and promote the winners. AgentCore Observability, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, provides visibility into agent behavior, and evaluations provide signals about agent quality. Together, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/optimization-recommendations.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;recommendations&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/configuration-bundles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;configuration bundles&lt;/a&gt;, and validation through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/ab-testing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;A/B testing&lt;/a&gt; provide a workflow for improving an agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system prompt optimizer in AgentCore uses agent traces recorded in AgentCore Observability together with a reward signal to produce an improved system prompt. For the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/02-use-cases/01-conversational-agents/market-trends-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;market trends agent&lt;/a&gt;, the recommendation compares an existing system prompt with a revised version and explains the trace patterns that motivated the changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-1.png" alt="Original system prompt beside a recommended system prompt for the market trends agent" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: A recommendation compares the existing system prompt with a revised prompt for the market trends agent&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The recommendation interface also provides an explanation of the proposed changes, so you can review the reasoning before deciding whether to test the recommendation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-2.png" alt="Explanation panel accompanying a system prompt recommendation" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: An explanation accompanies the recommended system prompt to support review and validation&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post is a technical companion to the launch post. We take a deeper look at the system prompt optimizer and explain how its recommendation engine works. We also share evaluation results for the Single Agent Reflector and the experimental, open source Sub-Agent Reflector on two publicly available benchmarks, alongside &lt;a href="https://github.com/gepa-ai/gepa" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GEPA&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://dspy.ai/api/optimizers/MIPROv2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MIPROv2&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="optimizer-design"&gt;Optimizer design&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system prompt optimizer is executed by an agentic reflector, the reasoning component of the optimizer. It reviews evaluated agent behavior, identifies patterns that distinguish successful runs from failures, and proposes targeted changes to the agent configuration. In the system-prompt optimization workflow, its primary output is a revised system prompt accompanied by an explanation of why the proposed changes should improve agent quality.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agent traces are often lengthy, and even a few dozen can quickly exceed a model’s context window. Passing every trace directly into the reflector prompt is therefore impractical. Instead of truncating or pre-summarizing traces to fit, the complete trace corpus is made available to the reflector through a filesystem.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The design uses a minimal workflow. An evaluator scores a collection of traces and writes them to a directory accessible to the reflector agent. The reflector is given a shell tool, directed to that directory, and instructed to propose updates to the agent configuration by examining patterns of success and failure across the traces.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The reflector can inspect the corpus as needed. It can list files, search with &lt;code&gt;grep&lt;/code&gt;, read traces with &lt;code&gt;cat&lt;/code&gt;, compare outputs with &lt;code&gt;diff&lt;/code&gt;, and selectively examine successful and unsuccessful runs. We don’t impose a fixed signal-extraction or trace-summarization pipeline. Instead, the reflector determines which evidence matters, what comparisons to make, and how to translate those findings into configuration changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;It then returns proposed edits to the agent configuration. Before any proposal can be applied, it must pass through platform-level guardrails.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Responsible AI considerations are part of this workflow. Recommendations should be reviewed and tested before use, and the guardrails described later in this post screen candidate updates before they can be promoted.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="single-agent-reflector"&gt;Single agent reflector&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Single Agent Reflector drives system prompt recommendations in AgentCore optimization today. One reflector agent works through the full trace set in a single pass. It surveys the score distribution, drills into the most informative segments of individual traces, contrasts successes with failures, and returns one coherent set of edits to the agent configuration. Each optimization epoch repeats this cycle: score the traces, reflect, and accept the edits that pass the guardrails. You can run additional epochs to trade optimization time for further quality gains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 3 shows this flow. Evaluated traces are written to a shared directory, where a single reflector inspects the evidence and produces recommended configuration edits. The guardrails screen those edits before they’re accepted.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="mceTemp"&gt;&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_139563" style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-139563" loading="lazy" class="wp-image-139563 size-full" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/single-agent-reflector-800w.png" alt="" width="800" height="718"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-139563" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: The Single Agent Reflector analyzes evaluated traces and returns guarded configuration edits&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="sub-agent-reflector"&gt;Sub-Agent Reflector&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Sub-Agent Reflector extends the Single Agent Reflector. It trades some efficiency for a higher quality ceiling by using a &lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com/docs/user-guide/concepts/multi-agent/swarm/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;swarm&lt;/a&gt; of agents to explore different parts of the trace set dynamically. A single reflector pass can focus on patterns in the first 5–10 traces it examines and miss failure modes that appear only in a smaller portion of the corpus.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each sub-agent performs a three-level analysis on a single trace:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Surface:&lt;/strong&gt; Extract the reward, difficulty, and outcome.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Turn level:&lt;/strong&gt; Locate the decision point where the trajectory diverged from the optimal path, using shell commands such as &lt;code&gt;jq&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;grep&lt;/code&gt; to parse rollout JSON.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cognitive:&lt;/strong&gt; Diagnose why the agent failed at that decision point and provide reflective guidance.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each sub-agent returns a concise finding and a corrective rule. Because each sub-agent operates in its own context window, it can focus on one trace without being influenced by the others. The orchestrator aggregates the findings, generalizes recurring patterns, removes duplicates, and condenses the resulting insights into configuration changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 4 shows the multi-agent flow. The orchestrator delegates individual trace analyses to sub-agents, collects their findings, and synthesizes the findings into a single set of guarded configuration edits.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div id="attachment_139563" style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img aria-describedby="caption-attachment-139563" loading="lazy" class="size-full wp-image-139563" src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/16/single-agent-reflector-800w.png" alt="" width="800" height="718"&gt;
 &lt;p id="caption-attachment-139563" class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: The Sub-Agent Reflector uses independent trace analyses to inform guarded configuration edits&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We have launched this experimental feature as a preliminary release in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/strands-labs/harness-optimizer/tree/main" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands open-source GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="guardrails"&gt;Guardrails&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Naive configuration optimization can drift in predictable ways: prompts can grow longer after optimization, prompts can quote terms from traces as examples, and safety constraints can be relaxed or softened in pursuit of an evaluator score. We apply rubric-based guardrails to every candidate update before it is accepted:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Length ceiling.&lt;/strong&gt; A candidate that grows the configuration by more than 20 percent over the previous version is rejected, and the optimizer is asked to tighten it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Safety check.&lt;/strong&gt; Candidates are screened against safety standards before promotion.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;No verbatim phrases from traces.&lt;/strong&gt; Candidates can’t reuse exact phrases from the traces against which they were optimized. This helps prevent overfitting to surface features of the training traces.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="evaluation-results"&gt;Evaluation results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We evaluated the Single Agent Reflector and Sub-Agent Reflector alongside two established baselines, &lt;a href="https://github.com/gepa-ai/gepa" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GEPA&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://dspy.ai/api/optimizers/MIPROv2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;MIPROv2&lt;/a&gt;, on two public benchmarks: &lt;a href="https://appworld.dev/appworld/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AppWorld&lt;/a&gt; and &lt;a href="https://webshop-pnlp.github.io/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;WebShop&lt;/a&gt;. For each method, we swept the configuration space and reported the best result with its number of turns and wall-clock optimization time.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two patterns stand out:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Single Agent Reflector offers the best quality-per-cost trade-off.&lt;/strong&gt; On AppWorld, it reaches 81.55 percent in 6 minutes and 20 turns, an 18× speed-up over GEPA and a 36× speed-up over MIPROv2 for a comparable or better score. On WebShop, it reaches 78.31 percent with 5 turns and 1 minute of wall-clock time. This variant is well suited when you want fast iteration cycles.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sub-Agent Reflector achieves the highest quality on both benchmarks.&lt;/strong&gt; On AppWorld, it reaches 95.83 percent, a 23-point lift over the baseline and 16 points above the next-best method. On WebShop, it reaches 79.15 percent, a 4-point lift over the baseline. The per-trace sub-agent analysis has the greatest effect on AppWorld, where failure modes are diverse and a single reflector pass can miss minority patterns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These results reflect the two designs described earlier. Whole-set reflection gives the Single Agent Reflector its efficiency: it requires far fewer turns and minutes than iterative methods to reach competitive scores. Sub-agent decomposition gives the Sub-Agent Reflector a higher quality ceiling by analyzing each trace independently before aggregating the findings. This approach helps it identify failure modes that a single pass over many traces can miss.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 5 summarizes the AppWorld and WebShop comparison across the four methods. The Sub-Agent Reflector achieves the highest success rate, and, across both AppWorld and WebShop, the Single Agent Reflector and Sub-Agent Reflector outperform the GEPA and MIPROv2.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-5.png" alt="AppWorld success rates and optimization times for GEPA, MIPROv2, the Single Agent Reflector, and the Sub-Agent Reflector" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Performance comparison on AppWorld and WebShop across the four methods&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Figure 6 compares the number of turns consumed and the wall-clock optimization time across the four methods. The Single Agent Reflector is the most efficient option, using the fewest turns and the least wall-clock time on both benchmarks. The Sub-Agent Reflector also maintains a competitive optimization cost. On AppWorld, it uses the same 100 turns as GEPA and MIPROv2 and completes faster than MIPROv2. On WebShop, its approximately 20-minute wall-clock time is less than half that of either baseline, despite its more extensive trace analysis.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/09/09/ML-21426-6.png" alt="WebShop rewards and optimization times for GEPA, MIPROv2, the Single Agent Reflector, and the Sub-Agent Reflector" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: Turns consumed and wall-clock optimization time on AppWorld and WebShop across the four methods&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;Table 1: Best result per method on each benchmark, with the configuration that produced it. Baseline scores are shown in parentheses, and bold indicates the highest score per benchmark. For each benchmark, we report the headline metric, the configuration, total turns, and wall-clock optimization time. Configurations are &lt;code&gt;n_samples × epochs&lt;/code&gt; for the Single Agent Reflector and Sub-Agent Reflector and &lt;code&gt;n_samples × iterations&lt;/code&gt; for GEPA and MIPROv2. We scanned &lt;code&gt;n_samples&lt;/code&gt; from 5–50 and epochs or iterations from 1–10. The reflector model was Opus 4.6, and the task model was Sonnet 4.5.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="best-practices-and-looking-forward"&gt;Best practices and looking forward&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Start with a small trace set.&lt;/strong&gt; In our experience, 10–50 diverse traces can provide a useful starting point for optimization. Start small and grow the set only if the recommendations are too narrow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use more than a scalar reward when appropriate.&lt;/strong&gt; The reflector reads traces as files, so any signal written into a trace is available to it. Alongside the scalar score, free-text feedback, such as an evaluator rationale, a human annotation, or a user complaint, can be included in the same trace file for reflection.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Review and validate recommendations.&lt;/strong&gt; Use the recommendation explanation, offline evaluations, and controlled A/B testing to assess a proposed configuration before promoting it to production.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Use independent trace analysis for diverse failure modes.&lt;/strong&gt; The experimental, open source Sub-Agent Reflector analyzes traces independently before aggregating the findings. Consider this approach when different subsets of traces reveal distinct failure patterns.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Extend the analysis beyond system prompts.&lt;/strong&gt; Trace evidence can also identify opportunities to improve tool descriptions and reusable skills. Apply the same guardrails and validation workflow when evaluating these types of configuration changes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Start with the managed optimization workflow.&lt;/strong&gt; For most production use cases, AgentCore optimization provides the recommended path from traces to recommendations and validation. Teams with specialized research or integration constraints can apply the same trace, evaluation, and validation pattern to a custom reflection workflow.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AgentCore optimization connects trace-based evidence to recommended configuration updates and validation workflows. To get started, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore detail page&lt;/a&gt;, read the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/optimization.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;optimization documentation&lt;/a&gt;, open the &lt;a href="https://console.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/home#" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console&lt;/a&gt;, and explore the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/02-use-cases/01-conversational-agents/market-trends-agent" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AgentCore samples repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p style="clear: both"&gt;&lt;/p&gt;
